19 Agent Economics

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Agent stack focus for this chapter.

Why can a model with a tenth of the token price make an agent cost more for every task it finishes?

A model whose tokens cost a tenth as much looks like a large saving on the provider’s invoice and can still raise the price of every task the agent finishes. Token charges are only part of the account. An attempt also holds a sandbox open while its tools run, invokes tests and external services, waits for shared capacity, and may end in human review, and an attempt that fails pays for all of it without producing anything that ships. A cheaper model that needs more turns, retries more often, or passes assessment less often spreads those charges across fewer accepted tasks, so the bill per accepted result can rise while the token bill falls. The account that matters is kept over a cohort of tasks under one declared contract. It charges everything every attempt incurred, failed and canceled work included, to the tasks that contract accepted, and it keeps work still pending visible rather than dropping it. Reuse, retries, faster execution, model routing, added capacity, and delegation are each worth buying only when they lower that ratio or meet a deadline the objective values, and which lever matters depends on which term of the account dominates. Capacity enters the same account, because concurrent trajectories hold model servers, sandboxes, and reviewers while they wait, and an undersized pool turns waiting into cost. Horizon, state, and authority each set the closure a task must pay for, and an agent service is sustainable only when the accepted work it delivers covers that price.

Learning Objectives
  • Calculate cohort cost per accepted task while distinguishing attempt costs, human outcomes, and unsettled liabilities
  • Evaluate prefix caching, compaction, and retry purchases under explicit billing and acceptance assumptions
  • Diagnose which acceleration shortens a trajectory enough to pay for itself from the model’s share of its measured critical path
  • Evaluate model cascades under a common acceptance contract, including assessment costs and false-accept risk
  • Size resource occupancy for a target trajectory goodput (accepted tasks per unit time) and compare provisioning choices under declared workload, quota, and queueing assumptions
  • Design atomic budget reservations that preserve spending bounds across concurrent work and late settlement
  • Select a loop cap or architecture by pricing automated and human-completed outcomes under one acceptance contract
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

19.1 The Bill Behind an Accepted Repair

A team running the repair agent (Four lighthouse workloads) is offered a model whose tokens cost a tenth as much. On the provider’s invoice the switch looks like a large saving. In the team’s own accounts it can raise the price of every repair that ships. The cheaper model may need more turns to reach a passing patch, and each turn holds a sandbox open and invokes tools. More tickets may fail assessment, and each failure still paid for its model calls, its environment, its checks, and the engineer who triages it. The token discount is real, but it applies to only part of the account.

Horizon, state, and authority each set the closure a task must pay for.

Offers like this one recur, because the price of a fixed capability falls quickly. Epoch AI (2025) track the cheapest model that reaches a fixed score on each of several benchmarks, and figure 1 plots three of those price series.

Across all 21 capability thresholds in the source, the fitted decline runs from 9× to roughly 860× per year, with a median of 46×. A falling token price shrinks only the inference terms of the attempt cost. Sandbox lifetime, verification, cleanup, and human review are priced per turn, per check, and per hour of attention, not per token. Each price cut therefore moves a larger share of the bill onto terms the provider’s discount never touches, and onto the acceptance probability in the denominator.

The runtime built so far can already say part of what the team needs. The evaluation discipline of Agent Evaluation decides which repairs count as accepted and how two policies are compared on matched tasks, and the harness’s budget ceilings stop any one task from spending without limit. Benchmarking Against a Single Agent joined the two once, deciding for one stated workload whether splitting a migration across workers repaid its coordination tax against a matched single controller. None of these says in general whether a policy is worth running. An accuracy figure reported without its cost cannot rank two designs, which is why cost-controlled evaluation reports the two jointly (Kapoor et al. 2024), and a ceiling that holds says nothing about how much of the spending under it bought accepted work.

↩︎ Per-task budget ceilings and the bounded-liability admission test that enforces them are established in Budgets and Ceilings.

What is missing is an account that joins spending to accepted outcomes. Reliability and Cost over a Horizon introduced cost per accepted task, the expected cost of an attempt divided by the probability that the attempt is accepted, as one of the book’s two quantitative fundamentals. Here that ratio becomes something a team can measure over a real cohort of tasks. Its numerator holds every charge an attempt incurs, and its denominator counts only outcomes accepted under one declared contract. Each lever the runtime controls (reuse, retries, acceleration, routing, capacity, and delegation) is worth buying only if it lowers the ratio or buys a deadline the objective values.

The account comes first, in section 19.2, together with the rules that keep failed, canceled, and still-pending work inside it. Section 19.3 prices what the runtime pays to carry context from call to call and how many attempts it should buy. Section 19.4 finds where faster execution actually shortens a task, and section 19.5 decides which model should attempt the work at all. Section 19.6 sizes the services that concurrent trajectories hold and wait on, and section 19.7 makes spending limits hold across delegated children and late bills. Section 19.8 then compares whole architectures under one contract, and section 19.9 prices all four lighthouse workloads to show that each is bound by a different term of the same account.

19.2 Cost per Accepted Task

Before the team can say which model repairs tickets more cheaply, it must fix what a repair is. A configuration scored against visible tests cannot be compared with one scored against a sealed suite, however carefully the invoices are tallied, because the two denominators count different things. Acceptance is defined only under an explicitly declared contract, checker, and execution environment. Isolating the effect of one policy choice (model selection, prompt construction, or tool scaffolding) therefore takes a matched comparison that holds the task contract, evaluation harness, and resource ceilings fixed while only the policy varies. The cost accounting also needs an explicit task cohort, the declared set of initiated tasks whose charges and outcomes it covers, and a cutoff timestamp. Together they decide which initiated tasks are under review, which outcomes count as accepted before the cutoff, and which operations, assessments, or invoices are still pending.

19.2.1 Whole-trajectory cost and the task account

Within that boundary, an invoice that tracks only model tokens misses the container lease, test suite, cleanup job, and engineer triage that an attempt of \(\TurnHorizon\) turns incurs alongside inference long after the final token leaves the model server. This chapter numbers turns from one, \(\TurnIndex=1,\dots,\TurnHorizon\), so that the index matches the turn counts in its worked examples. The whole-trajectory cost of an attempt decomposes as

\[\AttemptCost = \sum_{t=1}^\TurnHorizon \left(\TurnInputTokenCost^{(\TurnIndex)}+\TurnOutputTokenCost^{(\TurnIndex)}+\TurnToolFeeCost^{(\TurnIndex)}+\TurnSandboxCost^{(\TurnIndex)}\right)+\AttemptVerificationCost+\AttemptCleanupCost+\HumanReviewCost+\AttemptRuntimeOverheadCost.\]

Inside the turn loop, the input (\(\TurnInputTokenCost^{(\TurnIndex)}\)) and output (\(\TurnOutputTokenCost^{(\TurnIndex)}\)) terms capture inference pricing under the provider’s rate card, including tiered rates and prompt-caching discounts. Tool invocation fees (\(\TurnToolFeeCost^{(\TurnIndex)}\)) cover billable external API requests and data lookups. The sandbox charge (\(\TurnSandboxCost^{(\TurnIndex)}\)) prices the wall-clock lifetime of virtual machines or containers, including idle waits while external tools run. Outside the turn loop, verification (\(\AttemptVerificationCost\)) covers post-execution checks and test suites not already billed as sandbox time, and cleanup (\(\AttemptCleanupCost\)) pays for state reset, storage teardown, or compensating transactions. Whenever rejected attempts or policy escalations trigger human review, \(\HumanReviewCost\) tallies manual engineering time within the declared system boundary. Runtime cost (\(\AttemptRuntimeOverheadCost\)) apportions orchestrator overhead, scheduler queues, and shared network telemetry. Every cost item must map to exactly one category so that an end-of-turn integration test billed as active sandbox compute time is never double-counted under verification.

Definition 19.1: Whole-trajectory cost

Whole-trajectory cost is the total expense attributed to a single execution attempt across its model calls, environment runtime, validation assessment, and required finalization within a declared accounting boundary.

  1. Significance: It forms the numerator of cost per accepted task (\(\CostPerAcceptedTask\)), capturing every cost driver that standard per-token pricing hides: container provisioning, external API calls, test-suite grading, state teardown, and human escalation. Failed attempts accumulate these charges just as successful ones do.
  2. Distinction: Whole-trajectory cost measures the expense of an individual attempt, whereas cost per accepted task amortizes all attempted and discarded work across the surviving accepted tasks in the cohort.
  3. Common pitfall: Closing the accounts the instant the agent process emits its final token, ignoring trailing cloud resources, pending cleanup jobs, or downstream engineer triage.

A single initiated task may involve multiple sequential retries or parallel delegated branches. Its task account, denoted \(\TaskCost\), aggregates every attempt, failed branch, and abandoned speculative execution, along with the task’s assigned share of capacity and orchestration overhead. When a sub-agent branch crashes or is canceled, its incurred costs do not vanish; they remain permanently logged against the task.

In production environments, operations are rarely settled instantly. The harness’s reservations hold part of the allowance against work that has been admitted but not yet billed. Because these funds remain committed until all external side effects resolve, any snapshot taken at an intermediate cutoff must distinguish settled expenditures from outstanding liability, the reserved balance backing active, unbilled operations. The settled total serves merely as a lower bound on the final task cost until all pending obligations clear.

19.2.2 Cohort cost per accepted task

Once those task accounts settle across a cohort, averaging the cost of successful tasks alone would still hide every rejected, timed-out, or canceled trajectory whose bills the surviving repairs must carry. For a finite cohort of \(\InitiatedTasks\) initiated tasks with fully resolved accounting, let \(\AcceptedTasks\) represent the number of distinct tasks accepted under the declared contract. If an agent executes three successful retries on a single ticket, the system delivers exactly one accepted task; retries never inflate the denominator. For \(\AcceptedTasks>0\), the cohort cost per accepted task is

\[\CostPerAcceptedTask=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{\InitiatedTasks}{\TaskCost}_i}{\AcceptedTasks}. \]

The numerator aggregates every dollar expended across all \(\InitiatedTasks\) initiated tasks, whether they succeeded, crashed, timed out, or were rejected by validation. Keeping failed and canceled work in the numerator and pending verdicts explicit is exactly what trajectory goodput and the accepted task (principle 15) demand of any account in this book. Only accepted tasks count as delivered work, and every charge incurred on the way to them stays on the bill. (This chapter defines trajectory goodput formally, as accepted tasks per unit time, when it sizes capacity in section 19.6.1.) If zero tasks are accepted (\(\AcceptedTasks = 0\)), the ratio is undefined. A transparent system report must then present the total expenditure and the zero count directly rather than masking the failure behind an artificial floor. Whenever tasks or invoices remain in flight at the accounting cutoff, the report must flag the cutoff timestamp and qualify the calculation as provisional. An unexecuted test suite represents a pending task, not an immediate failure, just as an asynchronous cloud billing adjustment can shift \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) hours after the code artifact is finalized.

Definition 19.2: Cohort cost per accepted task

Cohort cost per accepted task is the total cost of every initiated task in a cohort (failed, canceled, and still-pending work included) within a declared accounting boundary, divided by the number of distinct tasks accepted under one declared contract, checker, and environment by a stated cutoff.

  1. Significance: It provides the empirical, measurable form of this book’s central economic metric \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\). Because the count of accepted tasks divides every item in the numerator, a drop in task acceptance inflates the entire system bill, whereas a token price cut discounts only the inference fraction.
  2. Distinction: Whole-trajectory cost measures the expense of an isolated attempt; cohort cost per accepted task loads all failed, canceled, and abandoned attempts onto the small fraction of work that actually passes inspection.
  3. Common pitfall: Computing the average cost of successful trajectories alone. This survivor bias ignores the discarded attempts whose compute and environment bills must be paid by the surviving accepted tasks.

When all tasks in a cohort have reached a terminal state, the cohort acceptance rate is \(\AcceptanceFraction = \AcceptedTasks / \InitiatedTasks\). Let \(\AcceptedTaskMeanCost\) denote the mean cost of an accepted task, and let \(\UnacceptedTaskMeanCost\) denote the mean cost of an unaccepted task (encompassing outright failures, policy cancellations, and timed-out executions). When both subsets are non-empty, partitioning the numerator yields

\[\CostPerAcceptedTask=\AcceptedTaskMeanCost+\frac{1-\AcceptanceFraction}{\AcceptanceFraction}\UnacceptedTaskMeanCost.\]

The cohort fraction \(\AcceptanceFraction\) counts tasks, whereas the per-attempt acceptance probability \(\AcceptanceProbability\) of equation 1.2 counts attempts. When every task gets exactly one attempt, the two coincide, and \(\AcceptanceFraction\) estimates \(\AcceptanceProbability\). When a task may retry, \(\AcceptanceFraction\) estimates task-level coverage, the probability that some attempt on the task is accepted, while the per-attempt planning form stays \(\mathbb{E}[\AttemptCost]/\AcceptanceProbability\). Section 19.3.3 connects the two for sequential retry.

Each accepted task carries the rejected ones, and that load explodes as acceptance falls.

The factor \((1-\AcceptanceFraction)/\AcceptanceFraction\) represents the rejection multiplier, the number of failed tasks that each accepted task must pay for. This expression is an exact accounting identity for the cohort, not an asymptotic model or an assumption that retries will eventually converge. As the acceptance rate \(\AcceptanceFraction\) falls toward zero, the multiplier grows without bound. Whether a steep discount on model tokens yields lower overall operating costs depends on both groups’ costs, which is why no universal threshold decides model selection in isolation. The concrete bug-repair scenario below prices this trade-off directly.

Napkin Math 19.1: The token-price fallacy in automated bug repair
Problem: Two hypothetical configurations each process the same 100 tickets, with one bounded attempt per ticket and the same acceptance contract. Every attempt, rejected or not, uses the fixed per-attempt usage that table 1 assumes for its configuration, and all outcomes and charges are settled at the cutoff. The environment charge covers tool use, sandbox holding, and cleanup; the assessment charge covers checking not billed as sandbox time, together with allocated runtime overhead. Which configuration costs less per accepted repair?

Math: Applying the rates and usage in table 1 gives model charges per attempt of $0.240 and $0.069. Adding the other charges gives $0.410 and $0.329, respectively. The complete cohorts therefore cost $41 and $32.888. Dividing each total by its accepted count gives $0.55 and $1.32 per accepted repair. The lower-token-price configuration costs less per attempt but 2.4× as much per accepted task.

Now expand both accounting boundaries to include $10 of engineer triage for each rejected attempt. The frontier configuration has 25 rejected tickets and the other has 75. Total costs become $291 and $782.888; costs per accepted task become $3.88 and $31.32, a ratio of 8.1×. Triage adds no accepted repairs; if engineers also completed tickets, that combined service would need its own named account.

Systems insight: With one attempt per ticket, a cheaper configuration wins only if its attempt cost falls by more than its acceptance does. Here acceptance falls by 66.7 percent, so the low-price configuration needed attempts at least that much cheaper. Its token charges fall by 71.3 percent, but its all-in attempt cost falls by only 19.8 percent, because its extra turns each pay the environment charge. Each of its accepted repairs also carries 3 rejected attempts, against 0.33 for the frontier configuration, so every charge that scales with rejections, such as triage, widens the gap.

Systems Perspective 19.1: Reading a cost-per-accepted-task number
The cost per accepted task is a cohort-level ratio of aggregate expenditures to accepted deliverables, evaluated at an explicit cutoff timestamp. Engineers must not confuse this ratio with the arithmetic mean of individual task expenses. In small cohorts, sample variability also prevents the ratio from serving as an unbiased estimator of the theoretical planning ratio \(\mathbb{E}[\AttemptCost]/\AcceptanceProbability\). To be actionable, any reported \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) figure must be accompanied by its full context: total cohort expenditure, accepted task count with statistical confidence bounds, end-to-end latency distributions, pending execution statuses, and outstanding liabilities. An isolated efficiency number shorn of these context markers cannot support valid comparisons between competing system policies.

A rigorous matched comparison holds the acceptance contract and maximum resource allowances fixed across configurations, rather than enforcing identical expenditures. A more aggressive search policy might intentionally spend more compute per ticket to meet stringent latency deadlines or maximize task coverage, provided the underlying system objective values those gains. Inverting this metric yields goodput per dollar (accepted tasks delivered per unit of currency, the per-dollar counterpart of the trajectory goodput defined in section 19.6.1), which keeps the same accounting boundary but reads it as throughput. In both formulations, acceptance confirms only that an artifact satisfied the automated contract within the designated test suite; it does not guarantee the complete absence of latent defects.

↩︎ Matched-budget comparison, which fixes the acceptance contract and resource envelope while each policy spends it differently, is established in Matched-budget comparison.

Decomposing the account this way shows where a lever can act before anyone pays for it. The dominant term varies across workloads, and section 19.9 prices all four lighthouses to show which term binds each. The levers the runtime controls most directly act on the inference term and on the number of attempts. In automated bug repair, re-sent prompt context makes up most of the inference charge, so prefix caching and context compaction act on that charge, while reasoning limits and retry budgets set how many model invocations an accepted task buys. Whether the inference term is the one worth attacking for a given workload is a separate question, and for the repair agent the answer turns out to be no.

19.3 Context Reuse and Retry Budgets

Across the turns of a repair, the same prompt prefix recurs call after call. Context engineering arranges the request so that the stable part comes first, and the serving system keeps the key-value (KV) attention state of that prefix so the next call need not recompute it. Reuse lowers cost, but it is not free, and it competes with two other purchases the runtime can make: compacting the context, which shortens the prompt but discards reusable state, and buying more attempts, which raises coverage but multiplies the bill. Each must be priced under the same acceptance contract before the runtime can tell which one lowers the cost per accepted task.

↩︎ The exact-prefix, execution-parameter, residency, and tenant conditions under which retained attention state may be reused are established in Prefix compatibility.

19.3.1 Pricing prefix cache hits

When an agent client retransmits the full conversation transcript on every turn while the provider’s serving cluster retains compatible KV state in memory across calls, the tokens sent on the wire diverge from the tokens recomputed on the server.1 Pricing multi-turn reuse therefore requires distinguishing between logical input, physical computation, and billed tokens. Over an execution trajectory of \(\TurnHorizon\) turns, one call each, let \(\SequenceLength^{(\TurnIndex)}\) represent the logical input length on call \(\TurnIndex\), \(\EligibleCachedPrefixLength^{(\TurnIndex)}\) its eligible cached prefix length, and \(\OutputLength^{(\TurnIndex)}\) its billed output length. Under a published tariff with per-token rates \(\InputTokenPrice\) for fresh input, \(\CachedInputTokenPrice\) for cached input, and \(\OutputTokenPrice\) for generated output, the total token charge is

1 Prefix Reuse: Serving systems make cross-request reuse practical by storing attention state in paged memory blocks that requests sharing a prefix can share (Kwon et al. 2023), and by indexing retained state in a radix tree so subsequent calls locate their longest cached prefix (Zheng et al. 2024). A discounted cached-input tariff reflects this underlying mechanism directly: only an exact, resident token prefix qualifies for the discount.

Kwon, Woosuk, Zhuohan Li, Siyuan Zhuang, Ying Sheng, Lianmin Zheng, Cody Hao Yu, Joseph Gonzalez, Hao Zhang, and Ion Stoica. 2023. “Efficient Memory Management for Large Language Model Serving with PagedAttention.” Proceedings of the 29th Symposium on Operating Systems Principles, 611–26. https://doi.org/10.1145/3600006.3613165.
Zheng, Lianmin, Liangsheng Ying, Zhenghao Yin, Cody Hao Liu, Eric Yu, Prakhar Patel, Zhipeng Yang, Anthony K. Joseph, and Ion Stoica. 2024. “SGLang: Efficient Execution of Structured Language Model Programs.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2312.07104, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2312.07104.

\[ \TrajectoryTokenCost=\sum_{t=1}^{\TurnHorizon}\left[\InputTokenPrice(\SequenceLength^{(\TurnIndex)}-\EligibleCachedPrefixLength^{(\TurnIndex)})+\CachedInputTokenPrice{}\EligibleCachedPrefixLength^{(\TurnIndex)}+\OutputTokenPrice{}\OutputLength^{(\TurnIndex)}\right]. \tag{1}\]

Commercial pricing models often incorporate additional dimensions, including cache-write surcharges, hourly retention fees, and minimum eligible prefix thresholds. In an append-only agent transcript, each turn re-sends the cumulative history alongside newly observed tool outputs and model generations. With an initial prompt of \(\BasePromptLength\) tokens and \(\TurnAppendLength\) tokens appended per turn, the logical input over the whole trajectory is the quadratic re-send total \(\PromptTokensSent\) of equation 2.9. Ideal prefix reuse charges only \(\FreshInputTokenCount=\BasePromptLength+(\TurnHorizon-1)\TurnAppendLength\) of that total at the fresh-input rate, once each for the initial prompt and every appended increment. Priced under the tariff, the total input bill is \(\InputTokenPrice{}\FreshInputTokenCount+\CachedInputTokenPrice(\PromptTokensSent-\FreshInputTokenCount)\). Because both \(\CachedInputTokenPrice\) and \(\TurnAppendLength\) remain positive, the quadratic growth term does not disappear; caching merely scales down its cost coefficient from \(\InputTokenPrice\) to \(\CachedInputTokenPrice\). These formulations account for billed input tokens, not the physical accelerator memory required to hold this reusable state across requests.

↩︎ The accelerator memory that retained attention state occupies is established in The Footprint of Retained Attention State.

Prefix hits require strict alignment between the incoming token sequence and the cached execution context, including weights and adapters, token positions, attention masks, and applicable multimodal inputs, together with residency and tenant access conditions (principle 5). Reusability breaks at the first altered token. Output sampling settings alone need not change already computed prefix tensors; any additional provider cache-key restrictions belong to the service contract. In production deployments, distributed request routing, memory pressure and cache eviction, and protracted tool execution times degrade realized hit rates. Economic models must therefore price observed, eligible hits under the actual service contract rather than assuming theoretical upper bounds.

Napkin Math 19.2: Pricing an append-only trajectory
Problem: An agent trajectory runs 30 turns, starting with an initial prompt of 8,000 input tokens and appending 1,500 tokens on each subsequent turn. Each invocation generates 200 output tokens. The provider tariff charges $3 per million fresh input tokens, $15 per million output tokens, and 10 percent of the fresh-input price for cached reads, with no separate write or retention fees. How much does ideal prefix reuse reduce the token bill for this trajectory?

Math: Cumulative logical input across all turns reaches 892,500 tokens. Under ideal prefix reuse, only 51,500 tokens are billed at the fresh-input rate. Input processing therefore costs $2.678 without reuse vs. $0.407 with prefix caching. Combining this with the constant $0.090 output charge yields total bills of $2.768 and $0.497, representing a 5.6× cost reduction.

Systems insight: Cache hit rate governs financial efficiency. Because historical context constitutes almost all of this trajectory’s input, even small reductions in hit rate produce large absolute billing increases. If 10 percent of the eligible prefix misses (for example, because a volatile timestamp or session identifier sits near the prompt header), the session cost increases by $0.227, raising the cached bill by 45.7 percent. Prefix caching modifies only the model inference term in effective cost (\(\CostPerAcceptedTask\)): it lowers the cost of carrying context across turns while leaving delivered inputs, outputs, and task acceptance unchanged.

19.3.2 The price of compaction

Unlike prefix caching, context compaction actively alters the input transcript. Replacing prior conversation turns with an aggregated summary shortens the active prompt, though it risks invalidating cached attention state. If summarization preserves a stable system prefix, subsequent requests can still reuse those initial blocks. To price the cache-invalidation penalty explicitly, consider the case of a complete cache miss: the first compacted invocation shares no reusable prefix with prior turns, but subsequent calls resume append-only prefix reuse from the compacted prompt. The baseline comparison retains the full uncompacted context over the same remaining turns, holding future token additions and ordinary output lengths constant. This analysis isolates the pure token price of compaction; whether a given summary preserves downstream task acceptance is evaluated separately using the context-policy evaluation of Evaluating Context Policies.

Let \(\SequenceLength\) be the input length at the moment of compaction, \(\CompactedContextLength < \SequenceLength\) the compacted length, and \(\RemainingCallCount \ge 1\) the remaining ordinary invocations, including the first compacted call. Set the two call sequences side by side. Keeping the context makes \(\RemainingCallCount\) ordinary calls, the first of which reads the cached history plus one fresh increment. Compacting makes one summarization call followed by \(\RemainingCallCount\) ordinary calls on the compacted prompt. Assume the summarization call reads exactly what the keep path’s first call would have read, at the same cached and fresh prices. Its input charge then equals that call’s input charge, and the two cancel when the paths are differenced. Ordinary outputs are equal call for call. What remains on the compaction side with no counterpart on the keep side is the summary’s generated tokens, \(\OutputTokenPrice{}\SummaryOutputLength\), and the first compacted call, which must send all \(\CompactedContextLength\) tokens as fresh input to rebuild server KV cache state. Relative to keeping the original context, the upfront investment \(\CompactionUpfrontCost\) is therefore

\[ \CompactionUpfrontCost=\OutputTokenPrice{}\SummaryOutputLength+\InputTokenPrice{}\CompactedContextLength, \]

which combines the output generation fee of the summary with the fresh-input cost of repopulating the cache. Each subsequent call saves \(\CompactionPerCallSaving=\CachedInputTokenPrice(\SequenceLength-\CompactedContextLength)\) under the assumption of equal incremental growth. The cumulative cost difference across \(\RemainingCallCount\) remaining calls is therefore:

\[ \CompactionPathCost(\RemainingCallCount)-\KeepContextPathCost(\RemainingCallCount)=\CompactionUpfrontCost-(\RemainingCallCount-1)\CompactionPerCallSaving, \qquad \CompactionPaybackCallCount=1+\left\lceil\frac{\CompactionUpfrontCost}{\CompactionPerCallSaving}\right\rceil \quad(\CompactionUpfrontCost>0,\ \CompactionPerCallSaving>0). \]

Here \(\CompactionPaybackCallCount\) represents the payback horizon: the minimum number of remaining invocations required for per-turn cache savings to offset the initial compaction surcharge. When \(\CompactionUpfrontCost \le 0\), compaction pays for itself on the very first call; when \(\CompactionPerCallSaving = 0\) and \(\CompactionUpfrontCost > 0\), compaction never recovers its cost on token pricing alone. Accounting for the first compacted call separately from later turns prevents off-by-one errors when estimating the remaining trajectory budget.

Napkin Math 19.3: Count the first compacted call
Problem: In the preceding scenario, suppose the agent triggers compaction at turn 16, compressing 30,500 tokens down to 12,000. The summarization call emits 1,500 tokens under standard cached-history pricing. If 15 ordinary calls remain (including the first compacted turn), what is the minimum remaining horizon required for compaction to cost no more than retaining the full context?

Math: Retaining the uncompressed context incurs $0.24525 in remaining input costs. Compaction incurs $0.19035 across remaining ordinary calls plus $0.03570 for the summarizer, totaling $0.22605. The upfront differential is $0.05850, against which each subsequent turn saves $0.00555. With 11 calls remaining, compaction still costs $0.00300 more than baseline. Reaching 12 remaining calls yields a net saving of $0.00255.

Systems insight: Compaction lowers total expenditure only when the remaining trajectory length meets or exceeds the payback horizon \(\CompactionPaybackCallCount\). \(\CompactionPaybackCallCount\) scales inversely with the cached read rate, because the upfront cost \(\CompactionUpfrontCost\) depends on \(\InputTokenPrice\) and \(\OutputTokenPrice\), whereas the per-call savings \(\CompactionPerCallSaving\) are proportional to \(\CachedInputTokenPrice\). If the cached read tariff drops from 10 percent to 5 percent of the fresh-input price, the break-even horizon extends to 23 calls. Because only 15 calls remain, compaction under this discounted rate loses money. As KV caching becomes cheaper, compaction must occur earlier in an execution trajectory to justify its cache-invalidation penalty. Late-stage compaction must be justified by context window limits or attention fidelity rather than token savings. An aggressive summary that drops a key constraint and lowers acceptance erases any marginal token economy.

19.3.3 Reasoning and retry budgets

Caching and compaction lower the token bill of a single trajectory; purchasing deeper internal reasoning within a call or funding multiple execution attempts instead spends extra compute to raise task acceptance. Because a configured reasoning token limit acts as an upper ceiling rather than a fixed spend, financial accounting must track actual billed token consumption and its empirical effect on acceptance under an identical evaluation contract, while weighing serial deliberation tokens against added turn latency. Deeper reasoning raises the all-in attempt cost \(\MeanAttemptCost\) by the billed price of the extra tokens and pays for itself only if it raises the per-attempt acceptance probability \(\AttemptPassProbability\) by a larger proportion, so that \(\MeanAttemptCost/\AttemptPassProbability\) falls.

↩︎ The distinction between internal deliberation budgets and external orchestration is established in Internal budgets and external orchestration.

Consider a baseline model of sequential retry with early stopping, in which every attempt on a task has an identical acceptance probability \(\AttemptPassProbability > 0\). Conditioning on a fixed task prompt and external environment, attempts represent independent draws from the same agent configuration, each incurring an all-inclusive cost \(\MeanAttemptCost\) covering model inference, sandbox runtime, and output verification. Sequential retry terminates immediately upon the first accepted result or halts after exhausting a budget of \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) attempts. The overall task acceptance probability (or coverage) is \(\TaskCoverageProbability_k = 1 - (1 - \AttemptPassProbability)^\IndependentAttemptCount\), and the expected number of executed attempts is \(\sum_{j=0}^{\IndependentAttemptCount-1}(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^\SequentialAttemptIndex = \TaskCoverageProbability_k / \AttemptPassProbability\). Dividing the expected total spend by the expected accepted tasks yields \((\MeanAttemptCost \TaskCoverageProbability_k / \AttemptPassProbability) / \TaskCoverageProbability_k = \MeanAttemptCost / \AttemptPassProbability\). Under early stopping with independent trials, the expected cost per accepted task therefore does not depend on the attempt cap \(\IndependentAttemptCount\). Raising \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) expands overall task coverage, while early termination holds the average cost per successful outcome at exactly \(\MeanAttemptCost / \AttemptPassProbability\). This is the per-attempt form of equation 1.2 with \(\MeanAttemptCost\) as the attempt cost.

In contrast, launching all \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) attempts concurrently in a parallel batch costs \(\IndependentAttemptCount \MeanAttemptCost / \TaskCoverageProbability_k\) per expected accepted task, provided the evaluation harness can identify and return an accepted candidate from the pool. These are the sequential and batch costs of equation 13.6, with the generation and checking charges folded into one attempt cost \(\MeanAttemptCost\). What this chapter adds is the comparison between buying more attempts from a cheap configuration and buying one attempt from a stronger one.

Napkin Math 19.4: What a retry cap buys
Problem: A lightweight agent configuration achieves an acceptance probability of 0.30 at an all-in cost of $0.10 per attempt. A stronger configuration achieves 0.70 acceptance but costs $0.40 per attempt. Allowing up to 5 lightweight attempts under the independent-trials model, which policy achieves the lowest cost per expected accepted task: sequential retry with early stopping, an unconditional parallel batch of 5 attempts, or a single attempt from the stronger model?

Math: Sequential retry achieves an expected coverage of 83.2 percent while consuming 2.773 attempts per task. This produces an expected spend of $0.277 per submitted task and $0.333 per accepted task. Running all 5 attempts in parallel yields identical coverage but requires $0.500 per submitted task, increasing the cost per accepted task to $0.601. A single invocation of the stronger model costs $0.571 per accepted task.

Systems insight: Under early stopping, sequentially retrying a cheaper model outperforms a single call to a stronger model whenever \(\CheapConfigurationAttemptCost/\CheapConfigurationPassProbability < \StrongConfigurationAttemptCost/\StrongConfigurationPassProbability\). In this scenario, sequential retry also delivers superior aggregate coverage (83.2 percent vs. 70 percent). Executing the batch concurrently secures that same coverage at a 1.8× multiplier in cost per accepted task, the ratio of \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) to the expected sequential attempt count. A parallel burst is justified only when strict wall-clock deadlines assign significant value to lower latency, offsetting the substantial financial premium.

Production workloads rarely have a single, uniform success probability \(\AttemptPassProbability\). Easy tasks often pass on the first try and stop early, whereas difficult tasks maintain persistently low acceptance rates across repeated attempts. Because early stopping removes the easy tasks after one or two tries, later retry rounds are spent almost entirely on the harder tasks that remain. Substituting a single population-average success rate into \(1-(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^\IndependentAttemptCount\) ignores this filtering effect and systematically overstates aggregate coverage. Because each additional retry round draws from a harder surviving pool with a lower acceptance yield, raising the retry cap also causes the realized cost per accepted task to drift above the single-attempt ratio \(\MeanAttemptCost/\AttemptPassProbability\). Repeated-sampling studies show this effect. On benchmarks with automated verification, task coverage (the fraction of tasks solved by at least one sample) continues to climb across multiple orders of magnitude of generated samples, scaling roughly log-linearly rather than saturating as a single-parameter model would predict (Brown et al. 2024). Environmental feedback, service outages, and shared mutable state further complicate fixed-probability assumptions. Engineers must therefore evaluate retry policies using outcome distributions measured per task over repeated runs.

↩︎ Per-task acceptance probabilities, and the distinction between a task and a repeated run of it, are established in Tasks and repeated runs.

Generating extra candidate solutions also introduces verification risk. As analyzed in Balancing pool size against checking cost, repeated sampling raises the chance of a false acceptance, where a flawed candidate satisfies test assertions without truly solving the underlying task. Passing an automated check provides domain-specific verification evidence rather than an absolute guarantee of correctness. Ultimately, prefix caching, context compaction, and retry policies govern what compute resources an agent runtime purchases; determining how those purchased operations dictate end-to-end latency requires analyzing the critical execution path.

19.4 Critical-Path Latency

Accelerating a model call rarely yields a proportional reduction in end-to-end task time. In realistic agent workflows, container provisioning, repository setup, test suite execution, tool I/O, and consensus validation often dominate total elapsed time. Evaluating an acceleration technique therefore requires an end-to-end view. It must measure how much wall-clock time the proposed change strips from the critical path to a declared endpoint, what compute and memory resources it consumes, and whether the accelerated execution preserves the required acceptance evidence. Tracing exposes where time actually goes, while economic accounting determines whether accelerating that specific span is worth the operational cost.

19.4.1 Dependency graphs and elapsed time

Reading elapsed time from a distributed trace is easy to get wrong: summing an enclosing parent span’s duration with the durations of its nested children double-counts time, summing concurrent sibling spans inflates apparent latency, and omitting queueing delays, network transit, or uninstrumented runtime pauses undercounts it. When profiling an agent runtime, uninstrumented intervals must remain explicitly accounted for as unexplained gaps rather than arbitrarily attributed to neighboring components.

↩︎ The trace analysis of span nesting and the critical path is established in Span overlap and latency attribution.

2 Recovering the Dependency Graph: The critical-path method and its float are introduced with agent traces in Span overlap and latency attribution. What pricing adds is the reconstruction step: distributed-systems work recovered the dependency model of production requests from existing component logs, precisely so that critical-path analysis could say which component’s speedup would shorten end-to-end latency (Chow et al. 2014). An agent trace needs the same reconstruction, from dispatch and join records rather than span nesting, before any acceleration is priced.

Chow, Michael, David Meisner, Jason Flinn, Daniel Peek, and Thomas F. Wenisch. 2014. “The Mystery Machine: End-to-End Performance Analysis of Large-Scale Internet Services.” 11th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation (OSDI 14), 217–31.

A span’s parent in a call tree denotes syntactic containment, which does not necessarily reflect an execution dependency. An agent runtime might spawn background log flushes or asynchronous cache warmers that outlive the caller without blocking the next step. Determining end-to-end latency requires constructing a directed acyclic execution graph based on true dispatch and join conditions. In this graph, a directed edge indicates that the successor task cannot begin until its predecessor completes. Along any path, the critical path counts only disjoint, non-overlapping intervals rather than inclusive parent spans alongside their children. Over such a graph, with vertex durations \(\VertexSlotTime(\DependencyGraphVertex)\) and source-to-sink paths \(\SourceSinkPathSet\), the critical path is the longest path, the quantity every acceleration below is priced against:2

\[\CriticalPathDuration = \max_{\pi \in \SourceSinkPathSet} \sum_{\DependencyGraphVertex \in \pi} \VertexSlotTime(\DependencyGraphVertex). \tag{2}\]

When the graph contains only mandatory computation, equation 2 establishes an idealized lower bound on completion time. The critical path matches the actual observed wall-clock interval only when the dependency graph incorporates real-world scheduling artifacts: thread scheduling delays, resource contention waits, queueing latencies, and sandbox initialization. Furthermore, the boundary of the graph depends on the system contract. Emitting an initial streamed token, evaluating a candidate patch against unit tests, and fully committing a validated pull request represent different completion endpoints, each with its own dependency sink.

A non-critical branch can shrink without moving elapsed time, and a critical one pays off only until a parallel branch becomes the longest. For pricing, the first case is not worthless, because a faster off-path branch may still free cluster capacity or reduce hosting costs. The second case means that every proposed optimization is priced against a recomputed critical path, not by subtracting isolated component savings from total elapsed time.

19.4.2 The payoff of faster inference

When sandbox setup and test execution take minutes while model calls take seconds, paying a premium for faster inference barely moves completion time. Bounding the maximum possible wall-clock gain from model acceleration before buying faster serving hardware prevents teams from optimizing a tiny fraction of the critical path.

Napkin Math 19.5: How much does faster inference buy?
Problem: In a short hypothetical repair episode, far shorter than the 30-turn trajectory priced in notebook 19.2, how much elapsed time does a 4× acceleration of every model call save?

Variables: All intervals execute sequentially, from runtime initialization through final sandbox snapshot export, without queueing, overlapping execution, or uninstrumented gaps. The durations listed in table 2 represent disjoint, non-overlapping sums rather than nested spans. The proposed acceleration modifies only inference latency, leaving model outputs, tool operations, test evidence, scheduling decisions, and finalization unchanged.

Math: If model execution occupies fraction \(\ModelTimeFraction\) of baseline duration \(\Time\) and accelerates by factor \(\AcceleratorGain \geq 1\), Amdahl’s law (The coordination tax) bounds the new duration: \(\AcceleratedEpisodeTime = \Time[(1-\ModelTimeFraction)+\ModelTimeFraction/\AcceleratorGain]\). This is the delegation bound of equation 18.1 with the overhead term set to zero and the worker count replaced by the acceleration factor; here the accelerated part is the model rather than the parallel work. In this episode, model work accounts for 9.4 percent of elapsed time. A 4× model acceleration reduces the episode to 290.46 s, saving 21.94 s for an overall speedup of 1.08×. Even instantaneous inference (\(\AcceleratorGain \to \infty\)) yields a whole-episode speedup of only 1.10× under these assumptions.

Systems insight: The theoretical speedup ceiling \(1/(1-\ModelTimeFraction)\) dictates whether paying for faster model inference can ever satisfy an operational deadline. Because this ceiling depends only on baseline profile measurements, engineers can evaluate its viability before investing in model optimization or specialized serving infrastructure. In this repair episode, halving test execution duration while maintaining identical coverage saves 122.8 s, far more than the 21.94 s gained from a 4× model acceleration, and it also lowers the sandbox leasing charges in \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\). When an engineering latency target exceeds what \(1/(1-\ModelTimeFraction)\) can theoretically deliver, engineering effort belongs on test execution and environment lifecycle optimizations. In non-linear, branching trajectories, this principle applies directly to the recomputed critical path of equation 2.

19.4.3 Span types and their levers

Once distributed traces identify the critical path, engineers must match each bottleneck span to the physical factors governing its duration and the levers that can compress it (table 3). Buying faster token decoding when container cold starts or serial tool calls dominate the critical path spends engineering budget without moving completion time.

When tool operations are independent, the runtime can dispatch them concurrently provided security policies, authorization boundaries, and resource reservations permit parallel execution. If three tool calls dispatch simultaneously without I/O contention and all three must complete before the agent continues, the synchronization join waits only for the longest operation rather than the sum of all three. However, concurrency gains diminish when tools contend for local resources, when dispatch is staggered, or when a downstream tool requires the output of an earlier call. In the sandbox domain, warm container pools, pre-baked filesystem snapshots, and persistent build caches shorten environment initialization spans, subject to strict isolation and teardown guarantees before each reuse.

↩︎ The reset contract that a pooled or snapshot-restored sandbox must satisfy before serving a new lease is established in The reset contract.

Environment preparation can overlap model generation if the runtime executes speculative setup within a sandboxed, isolated scope that commits no external side effects before the full model response is validated and authorized. Streaming tokens from a large language model (LLM) does not constitute an authorized execution intent. Even a speculative read operation might violate an access boundary or leak uncommitted state. If the model output subsequently alters the required tool call or aborts, the runtime must discard speculative work, release allocated resources, and record any incurred compute charges in the task account.

Early stopping alters the evidentiary value of an execution as well as its duration. In a validation pipeline, a single failing assertion in a mandatory test immediately proves that a candidate solution is unacceptable, allowing the runtime to short-circuit the remaining suite. Conversely, an early pass confirms only a single check and provides zero evidence regarding the remainder of the test suite. Test prioritization and streaming failure detection thus accelerate defect rejection during intermediate iterations. However, final task acceptance still demands full test coverage across the declared verification contract. Furthermore, canceling a running check does not instantly free resources. Active worker processes must unwind cleanly, and any intermediate compute costs remain charged until the underlying jobs settle.

↩︎ Cancellation as an asynchronous control action, whose job may finish or keep running after the request and leave cleanup obligations unsettled, is established in Job supervision and cancellation.

19.4.4 When speculative decoding pays off

When a draft model’s token proposals are frequently rejected by the target model, speculative decoding spends extra compute on drafting and verification while slowing generation below baseline. As the serving lever of table 3 that shortens the generating span itself, speculative decoding must clear two boundaries: one inside the model call and one on the critical path. Inside the call, single-stream decoding is memory-bandwidth bound under the serving assumptions of Estimating per-call hardware work, which leaves room to check several proposed tokens for roughly the price of generating one.

In speculative decoding, a lightweight draft model3 proposes \(\DraftTokenCount\) candidate tokens per speculation round, and the larger target model verifies them in a single forward pass, accepting the longest matching prefix and generating one continuation token of its own (Leviathan et al. 2023; C. Chen et al. 2023). Let the computational cost of generating one draft token equal a fraction \(\DraftTokenCostRatio\) of a standard target decoding step, and let \(\MeanAcceptedDraftLength\) denote the mean number of draft tokens accepted per round. If evaluating \(\DraftTokenCount\) proposed tokens in parallel costs approximately one target step, each round yields \(\MeanAcceptedDraftLength + 1\) verified tokens in the time equivalent to \(\DraftTokenCount\DraftTokenCostRatio + 1\) target decoding steps. The resulting decoding speedup for an isolated stream is

3 Draft-then-Verify Decoding: The idea predates large language models. Blockwise parallel decoding predicted several future positions at once and backed off to the longest prefix a scoring model validated (Stern et al. 2018). Speculative decoding moved the proposals into a separate small draft model and added an acceptance rule under which sampled outputs keep the target model’s distribution, which is why its savings are purely a cost and latency question rather than a quality trade.

Stern, Mitchell, Noam Shazeer, and Jakob Uszkoreit. 2018. “Blockwise Parallel Decoding for Deep Autoregressive Models.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 31 (NeurIPS 2018).

\[\SpeculativeDecodingSpeedup=\frac{\MeanAcceptedDraftLength+1}{\DraftTokenCount\DraftTokenCostRatio+1}. \tag{3}\]

Speculative execution accelerates the generation stream if and only if \(\MeanAcceptedDraftLength > \DraftTokenCount\DraftTokenCostRatio\), that is, when the average number of accepted draft tokens exceeds the amortized drafting cost. When acceptance falls below this break-even threshold, drafting overhead accumulates faster than verification saves time, actively degrading generation latency. Furthermore, the assumption that parallel verification takes no longer than a single decoding step holds only when single-stream generation is memory-bandwidth bound (principle 2). As concurrent serving loads increase and large batch sizes shift accelerator utilization toward compute saturation, evaluating \(\DraftTokenCount\) tokens concurrently incurs substantial arithmetic overhead, eroding the latency advantage.

This break-even condition describes arithmetic steps within an isolated model call. Under production load, several costs erode the predicted gain. The runtime must synchronize the draft and target models and manage their KV caches. It also discards drafts when early stopping cancels generation mid-trajectory, holds the draft model in accelerator memory that could otherwise serve concurrent requests, and schedules variable-length verification batches. The condition therefore has to be confirmed under representative traffic by complete-call latency percentiles, accelerator utilization, queueing delays, and end-to-end task completion rates measured against the baseline configuration. A high token acceptance rate \(\MeanAcceptedDraftLength / \DraftTokenCount\) is an intermediate serving metric; it does not by itself establish a gain at the trajectory level.

On the critical path, speculative decoding shortens only the decode portion of each model call; prefill, queueing, and other call overhead join the non-model spans in the fixed part of the trajectory. Its trajectory-level effect follows from substituting the decode-stream speedup \(\SpeculativeDecodingSpeedup\) for the acceleration factor \(\AcceleratorGain\) in the Amdahl bound of notebook 19.5. Here \(\ModelTimeFraction\) is the decode share of baseline elapsed time, so the trajectory-level speedup stays below \(1/(1-\ModelTimeFraction)\). The estimate below treats the model intervals of the serial episode as pure decode, which makes it an optimistic bound.

Consider a concrete deployment scenario where the draft model proposes 5 tokens per round at a relative cost of 0.08 per token. The break-even threshold requires the mean accepted draft count to exceed 0.4 draft tokens per round. If the draft model aligns well with the agent’s prompt distribution and achieves 3.6 accepted tokens per round, equation 3 yields a decode-stream speedup of 3.29×. Applying this factor to the decode subtotal under the zero-nondecode approximation within the serial episode of table 2 reduces total elapsed time from 312.40 s to 292.1 s, a modest 1.07× speedup for the overall episode. Conversely, if a complex reasoning task drops acceptance to 0.3 tokens per round, the system drops below break-even. Decode-stream performance then degrades to 0.93× of baseline speed, expanding total episode duration to 314.6 s. Drawn to scale, required tests and sandbox lifecycle operations form a non-model floor that no model acceleration crosses, so even instantaneous inference shortens this episode by less than a tenth (figure 2).

Accelerating a component on the critical path directly expands deadline headroom, whereas accelerating an off-critical operation primarily frees hardware capacity or lowers hosting costs. An optimization can justify higher operational expense if shaving critical-path latency satisfies a stringent user-facing service-level agreement (SLA). Conversely, an optimization that boosts raw token generation rate should be rejected if interconnect contention or server overhead erases those savings at the workflow level. Deploying speculative decoding in an agent system therefore depends on two boundaries. The serving engine must satisfy the micro-level condition \(\MeanAcceptedDraftLength > \DraftTokenCount\DraftTokenCostRatio\) under realistic production concurrency. The overall agent workflow must also have a decode fraction \(\ModelTimeFraction\) large enough to make the macro-level ceiling \(1/(1-\ModelTimeFraction)\) worth pursuing.

Checkpoint 19.1: Cost and time per accepted task

Before routing calls across models, check the account and the clock.

19.5 Model Cascades and Routing

Critical-path analysis shows where faster execution shortens a task, while routing decides which model configuration should attempt the work in the first place. Sending every ticket to a frontier model overpays on routine code transformations that a small, specialized model can complete at negligible cost, whereas sending every ticket to the small model fails on complex, ambiguous system bugs and multiplies retry and triage costs. A model cascade4 evaluates a task through a sequence of configurations, beginning with an inexpensive tier and escalating only when necessary; FrugalGPT formalized this architecture for language model APIs (L. Chen et al. 2023). The economic value of a cascade depends directly on three operational quantities: the fraction of requests resolved by cheaper tiers, the overhead of their failed attempts, and the difficulty distribution of tasks that escalate.

4 Generating Cascade: The verifier cascade of Staged Verifier Cascades already borrowed the classifier-cascade economics of cheap early rejection. A model cascade inverts the roles: each tier generates a candidate rather than only filtering one, so a task that escalates has paid for generation at every tier it reached, and the savings come only from a high early-exit rate.

Every exit from a cascade must satisfy the same declared acceptance contract. A schema check suffices for a purely syntactic transformation, but it cannot establish that a source patch preserves authentication behavior. An inexpensive tier never earns the right to dilute verification; a tier’s price neither strengthens nor weakens the contract. Passing an assessment gate yields an accepted candidate record, while publication and tool execution remain separate runtime decisions governed by effect authorization.

The price spread a router can exploit is wide, and it does not track capability closely. A long-horizon software benchmark runs each configuration five times on every task under a 20-hour budget per trial and reports the mean score, the best and worst of the five, and the average cost and duration of a trial (FrontierSWE 2026). Figure 3 plots those results against cost.

Cost per trial spans $9 to $1,030 across the 19 configurations, and a single trial runs from 1 to 19 hours. The highest-scoring configuration costs 10× as much per trial as the runner-up for 3 points, while the median gap between a configuration’s best and worst trial is 21 points. Price is therefore a poor proxy for score, and the variation between repeated trials is larger than the difference between the top configurations. That is the opening a router or cascade exploits, and it is also why each tier’s acceptance must be measured on the tasks that actually reach it rather than read from its price.

19.5.1 Direct routing and conditional escalation

Choosing a model tier before generation risks assigning a complex task to an underpowered model or a routine query to an expensive frontier model, whereas trying a cheap tier first always pays for the failed attempt whenever the task has to escalate. A direct router makes the upfront choice by inspecting incoming task features prior to generation. A cascade instead speculatively executes an initial attempt on an inexpensive tier, assesses the candidate against an acceptance contract, and escalates only when that attempt fails.

For a three-tier cascade, let \(\StageConditionalAcceptanceProbability_j\) denote the conditional probability of acceptance at tier \(\CascadeTierIndex\), given that the request reached tier \(\CascadeTierIndex\). Assume every task not accepted at a lower tier continues to the next tier, with no additional retry, cancellation, or stopping branch. The reach probabilities, the probability that a task arrives at each tier, are \(\StageReachProbability_1=1\), \(\StageReachProbability_2=1-\StageConditionalAcceptanceProbability_1\), and \(\StageReachProbability_3=(1-\StageConditionalAcceptanceProbability_1)(1-\StageConditionalAcceptanceProbability_2)\). This product reflects the probability chain rule rather than an assumption that tiers fail independently. Because later tiers receive only the tasks earlier tiers failed, a difficulty-filtered remainder, their conditional acceptance rates cannot be copied from full-workload benchmarks.

Let \(\CascadeTierCost_j\) be the mean generation and associated execution cost conditional on reaching tier \(\CascadeTierIndex\), excluding its gate, and let \(\CascadeGateCost_j\) be that gate’s mean cost. Let \(\CascadeTierDuration_j\) and \(\CascadeGateDuration_j\) be the corresponding mean durations. Real systems must budget for prompt caching, sandbox preparation, and environment cleanup within these terms rather than pricing output tokens alone. Linearity of expectation gives

\[ \mathbb{E}[\CascadeTaskCost]=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\StageReachProbability_j(\CascadeTierCost_j+\CascadeGateCost_j), \qquad \AcceptanceProbability=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\StageReachProbability_j\StageConditionalAcceptanceProbability_j. \]

For a serial cascade where all elapsed intervals are represented, the mean duration is

\[ \mathbb{E}[\CascadeTaskDuration]=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\StageReachProbability_j(\CascadeTierDuration_j+\CascadeGateDuration_j). \]

The final tier must pay for its verification gate as well. With deterministic stage durations, a task reaching every tier takes \(\sum_j(\CascadeTierDuration_j+\CascadeGateDuration_j)\); with variable durations and queueing, characterizing tail percentiles requires the measured empirical distribution. A policy that stops early or leaves assessments unresolved uses its actual reach probabilities and reports those outcomes separately.

The planning metric of cost per accepted task is \(\mathbb{E}[\CascadeTaskCost]/\AcceptanceProbability\), which amortizes the cost of every reached tier across accepted outcomes rather than crediting only successful paths. Evaluating a direct frontier policy requires measuring its baseline acceptance rate across the full target cohort, since its performance on that general distribution is a different quantity from its rate on the filtered escalation subset.

Napkin Math 19.6: Price the accepted result at every exit
Problem: In a workload of 100,000 code-editing tasks, should an engineering team deploy a multi-tier cascade or route all requests directly to a frontier model to minimize cost per accepted result, and what latency penalty does the cascade incur?

Variables: Each candidate output undergoes an identical verification check. In order of tiers, the cascade’s model invocation and execution costs (excluding gating) are $0.0004, $0.0020, $0.0600 per reached tier, with execution times of 0.25 s, 0.80 s, 4.50 s. Evaluating the verification gate costs $0.0010 and consumes 0.40 s at each reached tier, including the final one. These figures account for container setup, execution, and cleanup overhead. This baseline assumes zero queueing delay and immediate settlement upon completion.

The cascade resolves 60,000 tasks at tier 1. Of the 40,000 tasks advancing to tier 2, it resolves 28,000. Of the 12,000 tasks advancing to tier 3, it resolves 11,400. The resulting conditional acceptance rates are 60 percent, 70 percent, 95 percent, respectively. In comparison, a direct-to-frontier policy resolves 97,000 tasks on an identical cohort. This full-workload rate is an independent baseline parameter rather than an extrapolation from the cascade’s filtered residual. It is lower than the cascade’s total acceptance even though the cascade’s top tier is the frontier model. That is because the cheaper tiers solve some tasks the frontier model fails (the tiers’ failure sets differ), and every escalated task gets another attempt under the same gate.

Math: The cascade successfully resolves 99,400 tasks (99.4 percent) and leaves 600 unresolved. Across the entire cohort, the cascade expends $992, which is $0.00992 per initiated task and $0.00998 per accepted result. By contrast, direct frontier routing expends $6,100, or $0.06289 per accepted result. Lower-tier cascade attempts must be paid for regardless of whether their candidates pass verification.

Under these deterministic execution times, the cascade averages 1.718 s per task, but tasks that fail all the way through require 6.75 s. Direct frontier routing requires 4.90 s, including verification.

Systems insight: Direct frontier dispatch costs 6.3× as much per accepted result as the cascade because 60 percent of all tasks exit at tier 1, where execution and verification costs represent a negligible fraction of a frontier call. The cascade’s higher acceptance contributes only the ratio of the two accepted counts, 99,400 to 97,000. Nearly all of the gap is spend per initiated task. The cascade trades latency for cost. Tasks that escalate through all tiers accumulate only $0.0044 in lower-tier tolls, but they incur 6.75 s of serialized latency compared to 4.90 s under direct dispatch. Because cost savings scale with the early exit rate, the key architectural lever is hybrid routing. Dispatching tasks predicted to fail early stages directly to the frontier tier eliminates the latency penalty for time-sensitive tasks while capturing cascade savings across the remaining workload.

The saving comes from the shape of the filter rather than from any one tier’s price. Reach falls tier by tier because accepted results exit where they are produced, while every reached gate charges its assessment whether or not its candidate passes (figure 4). The same structure explains the latency penalty, since a task that escalates through every tier pays every gate’s duration in series.

19.5.2 Gate errors and the decision to continue

When a cheap tier-1 lint check accepts a patch that breaks authentication in production, the cascade cost log records a sub-cent exit while the engineering team pays to diagnose and roll back an outage. Cost accounting records gate verdicts even when a gate is mistaken, because an observed accepted result is not automatically a correct result across every operational dimension. To price downstream consequences, define \(\text{FA}\) as the event that an initiated task is accepted by the gate yet violates an independently assessed operational criterion. Define \(\FalseAcceptHarmCost\) as its additional harm or remediation cost not already counted in the execution costs. The expected cost per initiated task including this risk term is

\[ \mathbb{E}[\HarmInclusiveTaskCost] =\mathbb{E}[\CascadeTaskCost]+\Pr(\text{FA})\,\mathbb{E}[\FalseAcceptHarmCost\mid \text{FA}]. \]

The harm term complements the cost per accepted task rather than redefining it, with its probability evaluated over initiated tasks. If an engineering team measures a false-accept fraction solely among accepted results, they must multiply that fraction by the overall acceptance probability to recover \(\Pr(\text{FA})\). Similarly, the false acceptance rate of How base rates limit check precision, evaluated exclusively among invalid candidates, relies on a different conditioning denominator. Catastrophic or irreparable consequences must remain constrained by strict operational authority and safety limits, regardless of their expected monetary value.

When escalating across tiers, the runtime should pass bounded diagnostic evidence and the identity of the failed candidate, treated as untrusted input, rather than forwarding the full conversational transcript. Forwarding full failure transcripts inflates prompt token spend at the expensive tier and risks biasing the stronger model to repeat earlier mistakes. The orchestration layer must also distinguish pending or unavailable assessments from definitive rejections, ensuring that transient infrastructure faults do not trigger unnecessary tier escalation. A passing assessment yields an accepted-candidate record; publication still verifies current dependencies, operational authority, and required effect authorization before applying changes.

A learned router can estimate the probability and cost of each eligible remaining policy from offline evaluation data, for example by training on preference comparisons between stronger and weaker models (Ong et al. 2024). However, training records reflect only the configurations that were actually executed; logs collected under a selective routing policy omit counterfactual outcomes for untried alternatives.5 Whatever the estimates come from, the router applies one decision rule. For a simple monetary objective with acceptance probabilities \(\BaselinePolicyAcceptanceProbability,\CandidatePolicyAcceptanceProbability\), expected additional costs \(\BaselinePolicyCost,\CandidatePolicyCost\), and outcome value \(\AcceptedOutcomePayoff\) (with rejected outcomes valued at zero), policy \(\CandidateRoutingPolicy\) is economically preferable whenever

5 Counterfactual Logs: Learning from the logs of a deployed selection policy is an off-policy causal inference challenge, studied extensively in online ad placement where a platform observes outcomes only for selected actions (Bottou et al. 2013). Estimating what an alternative policy would have achieved requires that the logging policy explored with known, nonzero probabilities: if a router never sends an easy-looking task to the frontier tier, the historical logs contain no empirical evidence about what that frontier route would have achieved.

Bottou, Léon, Jonas Peters, Joaquin Quiñonero-Candela, Denis X. Charles, D. Max Chickering, Elon Portugaly, Dipankar Ray, Patrice Simard, and Ed Snelson. 2013. “Counterfactual Reasoning and Learning Systems: The Example of Computational Advertising.” Journal of Machine Learning Research 14: 3207–60.

\[ \AcceptedOutcomePayoff(\CandidatePolicyAcceptanceProbability-\BaselinePolicyAcceptanceProbability)>\CandidatePolicyCost-\BaselinePolicyCost. \]

For direct routing, this decision rule compares two complete end-to-end policies before execution begins. After an initial cheap attempt fails, however, the router compares the remaining escalation policy against stopping or invoking human fallback, treating already incurred spend as a sunk cost. A cheap-then-frontier policy carries its own reach-weighted cost and compound duration, not merely the cheap model’s nominal price. In all cases, the decision rule must operate inside remaining budget, deadline, and operational authority limits; positive expected value never licenses spending past a hard limit or substitutes for explicit authorization.

System load further reshapes this comparison. Waiting for an overloaded model tier consumes wall-clock time and holds open active execution environments, such as containerized sandboxes. When the queue for an inexpensive tier backs up, routing a task directly to an idle frontier model can yield lower total cost and lower wall-clock latency, even at a higher nominal inference price. Production routers must therefore incorporate real-time queue depths and resource contention metrics into their execution-cost and completion-time estimates, linking dispatch decisions directly to underlying queuing and service capacity models.

19.6 Capacity for Trajectories

Admitting one 20-minute repair trajectory per minute holds 20 sandboxes open concurrently while sending 60 model calls per minute to the inference pool. Sizing an agent platform therefore differs from sizing a stateless web service. Work enters the platform as a multi-step trajectory, but the operations that saturate hardware consist of individual model calls, tool executions, and resource leases, each with its own arrival rate, holding interval, and concurrency limit. A routing policy shapes the traffic arriving at each downstream model, tool sandbox, and external API, and capacity planning determines whether those services can sustain the offered load within strict latency and resource budgets.

19.6.1 Residence, service, and quota

A trajectory suspended for five minutes on an integration test or human approval consumes zero model-accelerator cycles while continuing to occupy a container lease and retained KV-cache memory. Because each resource is held over a different interval, a single concurrency counter cannot size the platform. In this capacity model, each admitted trajectory executes one distinct task policy, including its internal retries and delegated calls. For a stable population with finite long-run averages, Little’s law (theorem 5.1) relates the mean number of admitted trajectories present, \(\ConcurrentTrajectories\), to their admission rate \(\TrajectoryAdmissionRate\) and mean time from admission to departure, \(\mathbb{E}[\TrajectoryDuration]\):

\[\ConcurrentTrajectories = \TrajectoryAdmissionRate\mathbb{E}[\TrajectoryDuration]. \tag{4}\]

↩︎ Little’s law, and the residency tax that a tool wait imposes on KV-cache occupancy, are established in Residence time and service rate.

Applying Little’s law requires rigorous accounting of system boundaries. Trajectories rejected at admission never enter the system population, whereas admitted trajectories that eventually fail or abort contribute fully to concurrency while active. Little’s law must be applied separately at each resource boundary (sandbox leases, retained key-value cache memory, active model inference workers, and pending external API calls), with the arrival rate and holding interval that govern that boundary.

↪ Little’s law applied per resource boundary to trajectories with tool waits, contrasting live trajectories with the attention-state memory they retain, is developed in Queueing for Trajectories.

Because Little’s law governs only long-run expectations, engineering a platform for high percentiles requires analyzing the underlying occupancy distributions. Peak concurrency and latency tail buffers depend heavily on state-retention policies and burst dynamics. Under stable flow, the overall demand for model calls depends on the average number of invocations per admitted trajectory, including internal retries, reflection loops, and delegated sub-agent calls: \(\ArrivalRate=\TrajectoryAdmissionRate\mathbb{E}[\TrajectoryCallCount]\). In practice, these model invocations rarely arrive as smooth Poisson streams. When an agent cohort encounters a shared external bottleneck or completes a parallel tool phase simultaneously, downstream inference clusters experience severe arrival bursts.

Task acceptance couples operational demand directly to the economic balance sheet. Delivering accepted tasks at goodput \(\TrajectoryGoodput\) when only an acceptance fraction \(\AcceptanceFraction\) of admitted tasks succeeds requires the platform to admit

\[\TrajectoryAdmissionRate=\frac{\TrajectoryGoodput}{\AcceptanceFraction}, \qquad \ServingUtilization(\AcceptanceFraction)=\frac{\TrajectoryGoodput\,\mathbb{E}[\TrajectoryCallCount]}{\AcceptanceFraction\,\WorkerCount\ServiceRate}\]

for a pool of \(\WorkerCount\) model workers, each completing \(\ServiceRate\) calls per unit time; \(\ServingUtilization\) is then the fraction of the pool’s call capacity the admitted trajectories demand. The trajectory goodput \(\TrajectoryGoodput\) is the primary deliverable of the platform; failed or rejected tasks consume scarce cluster capacity without producing realized value.

Definition 19.3: Trajectory goodput

Trajectory goodput is the number of distinct tasks accepted under a declared contract per unit of elapsed observation time.

  1. Significance: It represents the core throughput metric for sizing an agent platform. At acceptance fraction \(\AcceptanceFraction\), every unit of goodput requires admitting \(1/\AcceptanceFraction\) trajectories, so acceptance governs both the spending multiplier in effective cost \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) and the offered server load \(\ServingUtilization(\AcceptanceFraction)\).
  2. Distinction: Token, call, and raw trajectory throughput measure computational work performed; trajectory goodput isolates accepted outcomes. Similarly, goodput per dollar divides the accepted count by total cost rather than elapsed observation time.
  3. Common pitfall: Counting redundant retries on a single task, or runs that complete outside the declared contract, as incremental goodput.

Sizing a concrete fleet from its trajectory admission rate means checking each resource boundary on its own unit of demand rather than looking for a single bottleneck counter. An admitted stream that fits comfortably inside its sandbox pool can still saturate its model workers or exhaust a provider’s token-per-minute allowance, as the steady-state fleet calculation below shows.

Napkin Math 19.7: One arrival rate, different resource demands
Problem: A hypothetical fleet has a trajectory admission rate of 1 per minute; an admitted trajectory remains present for 20 minutes on average. It makes 60 calls on average, each consuming 30,000 quota-accounted tokens on average across calls. In this scenario the provider counts input and output tokens, including cached input, toward a shared token-rate allowance of 2,000,000 tokens per minute. What mean concurrency and token demand does this imply?

Math: Apply equation 4 to admitted trajectories, and multiply the long-run call rate by mean tokens per call. The token average is call-weighted, not the unweighted mean of per-task averages.

Result: Mean trajectory concurrency is 20, while the mean call arrival rate is 60 calls per minute. The mean token demand is 1,800,000 per trajectory, giving a token rate of 1,800,000 tokens per minute and using 90 percent of the allowance. The quota alone permits a trajectory admission rate of at most 1.11 per minute at this workload mix in the long run.

Systems insight: The token quota sets the admission ceiling calculated here; concurrency, memory, sandbox, reviewer, and model-service limits still require their own checks. Holding goodput fixed, admissions scale as \(1/\AcceptanceFraction\), so a relative drop in acceptance of 10 percent pushes mean demand to the full allowance, leaving no headroom for bursts. A model or prompt change that lowers acceptance is therefore also a capacity change. Analyzing burst fit, deadline feasibility, and sandbox residency requires validating the quota’s actual window semantics against production traces on top of these steady-state means.
