Upstream provider quotas count requests, tokens, or concurrent connections across sliding windows of varying duration, and admission control must match the specific contract. A billing discount on cached input does not relax a token-rate limit, which still counts those tokens at full weight, so prefix caching lowers the bill without raising this admission ceiling. Within the platform, reserving capacity for active trajectories can prevent wasted partial work. Such admission and scheduling decisions must depend on forward-looking attributes: remaining resource requirements, deadlines, tenant fairness, and expected task value. Money already spent is a sunk cost and provides no justification on its own for prioritizing a trajectory.

↩︎ The gates that admit each model call against its provider’s quota windows are established in Quota and capacity gates.

19.6.2 The price of queueing

In a shared platform, an agent’s model calls compete in a serving pool alongside every other active trajectory. If the serving cluster runs at 70 percent utilization, pushing utilization to 90 percent lowers the amortized hardware cost per call. The resulting throughput gains appear immediately on the infrastructure bill. The accompanying queue delays do not, because waiting time accumulates silently across every serial step of an active trajectory while sandboxes, execution environments, and attention state remain held.

The simplest model that quantifies this trade-off is a nonpreemptive, first-come-first-served queue fed by a stationary renewal stream. Interarrival times are independent and identically distributed with mean \(1/\ArrivalRate\), and service times \(\RequestServiceTime\) are independent and identically distributed with finite variance. The sequences are mutually independent, with no batching, priority preemption, abandonment, or feedback. Let \(\InterarrivalVariationCoefficient^2\) and \(\ServiceTimeVariationCoefficient^2\) denote the squared coefficients of variation6 (variance divided by squared mean) of interarrival and service times, and let \(\ServingUtilization=\ArrivalRate\mathbb{E}[\RequestServiceTime]<1\) denote server utilization. For this single-server system, the Kingman approximation7 (Kingman 1961) estimates the mean waiting time before service begins:

6 Squared Coefficient of Variation: The scale-free measure of burstiness, defined as \(\IntervalVariationCoefficient^2 = \IntervalStandardDeviation^2 / \IntervalMean^2\). Exponential interarrival or service times, characteristic of a Poisson stream, have a squared coefficient of variation of exactly one, whereas perfectly periodic events yield zero. Values exceeding one describe arrivals and service durations burstier than Poisson, which is the standard profile for agent traffic arriving in cohorts with divergent prompt lengths.

7 Heavy Traffic: Kingman’s 1961 note showed that as load approaches saturation (\(\ServingUtilization \to 1\)), the steady-state waiting-time distribution of the single-server queue, appropriately normalized, converges to an exponential distribution (Kingman 1961). This property extends beyond the mean: near saturation, the entire waiting-time distribution, and with it every tail percentile, stretches by the factor \(\ServingUtilization/(1-\ServingUtilization)\), so percentile-based latency targets degrade just as rapidly as the mean.

Kingman, John Frank Charles. 1961. “The Single Server Queue in Heavy Traffic.” Mathematical Proceedings of the Cambridge Philosophical Society 57 (4): 902–4. https://doi.org/10.1017/S0305004100036094.

\[\mathbb{E}[\RequestQueueTime] \approx \frac{\InterarrivalVariationCoefficient^2+\ServiceTimeVariationCoefficient^2}{2}\frac{\ServingUtilization}{1-\ServingUtilization}\mathbb{E}[\RequestServiceTime]. \tag{5}\]

Near saturation, a little more utilization buys a lot more waiting.

Boon, Marko A. A., Augustus J. E. M. Janssen, and Johan S. H. van Leeuwaarden. 2023. “Heavy-Traffic Single-Server Queues and the Transform Method.” Indagationes Mathematicae 34 (5): 1014–37. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.indag.2023.02.004.

The factor \(\ServingUtilization/(1-\ServingUtilization)\) explains why queueing delays escalate steeply near full utilization. Equation 5 is a heavy-traffic approximation to the mean rather than an exact expectation or a percentile. Heavy-tailed queueing theory derives the corresponding Pollaczek–Khinchine waiting-time formula under general service distributions, and Boon et al. (2023) provides formal derivations and error bounds. Capturing correlated agent bursts, dynamic continuous batching, worker pools, and shared quotas requires queueing networks or trace-driven discrete-event simulations. Substituting an aggregate cluster service rate into a single-server formula does not turn it into a valid multi-server model. Two workable estimates remain for a pool. When a dispatcher pins each trajectory to one endpoint, every endpoint is its own single-server queue fed by its share of the arrivals, and equation 5 applies per endpoint. When workers share one queue, a trace-driven simulation of the pool, or the multi-server models in the further reading, replaces the single-server estimate.

Napkin Math 19.8: Pricing mean queue delay
Problem: In the hypothetical single-server model above, mean service takes 1 second, arrival variability \(\InterarrivalVariationCoefficient^2\) is 2, and service variability \(\ServiceTimeVariationCoefficient^2\) is 4. A waiting call holds resources costing $0.002 per second. How do estimated mean wait and holding cost change between 70 percent and 90 percent utilization?

Math: Apply equation 5, then multiply mean wait by the stated linear holding rate. The rate covers only resources retained during the queue interval, excluding model service, tool execution, and any separate valuation of user delay.

Result: Estimated mean wait rises from 7 seconds to 27 seconds, and the corresponding holding charge rises from $0.014 to $0.054 per call.

Systems insight: Moving from 70 percent to 90 percent utilization buys 28.6 percent more throughput from the same server, but it multiplies mean wait by 3.9× because the \(\ServingUtilization/(1-\ServingUtilization)\) factor dominates near saturation. While the holding charge for an isolated call appears small, this delay compounds across every serial step of an agent trajectory. Halving the variability term \((\InterarrivalVariationCoefficient^2+\ServiceTimeVariationCoefficient^2)/2\), for example by smoothing bursty admissions or standardizing prompt lengths, halves the wait at any utilization and provides a cost-effective lever before purchasing reserve capacity. Sizing that reserve ultimately depends on enforcing tail latency targets at measured percentiles.

A one-in-a-hundred delay per call becomes routine across a long trajectory.

Mean metrics also obscure how tail latency compounds across multi-step execution. If each of \(\SerialCallCount\) serial calls independently exceeds its 99th-percentile latency with probability \(\CallTailProbability\), the trajectory encounters at least one such delay with probability \(1-(1-\CallTailProbability)^\SerialCallCount\), which is the horizon compounding of equation 1.1 applied to latency rather than to correctness. For a trajectory of 30 serial calls, each with tail probability 0.01, the probability of experiencing at least one tail delay reaches 0.26. This is the probability of encountering a per-call tail event, not the probability of missing a trajectory deadline; the latter requires the complete duration distribution and the declared deadline. Sizing a fleet solely for acceptable per-call latency can therefore conceal end-to-end delays. This dynamic is the serial equivalent of the fan-out straggler problem in distributed systems (Dean and Barroso 2013). Correlated delays, such as a shared queue that remains backlogged across successive steps, alter the exact probability but not the operational conclusion. That conclusion is to provision and schedule against trajectory-level percentiles rather than per-call means (principle 15).

↩︎ The parallel counterpart, an all-results barrier exposed to fan-out stragglers, is established in Hedged requests and speculative work.

Integrating queueing delays completes the model-routing framework of section 19.5. A dynamic router compares eligible remaining paths, each priced with its queue and service delays, retained-resource costs, verification checks, and possible escalations, under an identical acceptance contract. Because dispatching decisions alter the arrival rates that produced the initial delay estimates, the router must refresh queue projections whenever traffic shifts. Separating interactive and batch pools, or enforcing priority scheduling, protects latency-bound work at a quantifiable cost in fairness and cluster utilization.

19.6.3 Dedicated capacity vs. per-token APIs

When an agent application’s token consumption becomes sustained and predictable, engineering teams must choose between paying on demand through per-token APIs and provisioning private infrastructure. Consider a dedicated deployment incurring a fixed infrastructure expense of $28 per hour plus $2 per million output tokens in marginal operating costs. Such private infrastructure represents dedicated capacity, a financial commitment paid continuously regardless of whether accelerators are busy or idle. The economic decision therefore turns entirely on sustained utilization.8 This comparison requires a strictly matched workload: equivalent task capability, acceptance criteria, data-privacy constraints, and feasible tail latency under representative traffic mixtures. A shared model architecture or nominal token rate alone does not establish equivalence.

8 Pay-as-You-Go Economics: Mirroring the foundational economic argument for cloud computing, renting capacity on demand can cost less than owning dedicated infrastructure even at a higher hourly rate, because owned capacity must be financed across its average utilization while utility pricing shifts provisioning risk to the provider (Armbrust et al. 2010). A per-token API carries that logic to its finest granularity.

Armbrust, Michael, Armando Fox, Rean Griffith, Anthony D. Joseph, Randy Katz, Andy Konwinski, Gunho Lee, et al. 2010. “A View of Cloud Computing.” Communications of the ACM 53 (4): 50–58. https://doi.org/10.1145/1721654.1721672.

Fix the workload’s input/output token ratio and prompt-cache hit rate. Because dedicated capacity is sized by its output generation rate, we put the API tariff on the same per-output-token basis by folding each output token’s associated input charges into a single effective price \(\EffectiveApiOutputTokenPrice\). This effective price adds to the API’s output-token rate the workload’s input-to-output token ratio multiplied by the average input price, where that average blends the uncached and cached input rates by the prompt-cache hit rate. Let \(\DedicatedVariableTokenCost\) be the variable cost per output token on dedicated capacity, and let \(\DedicatedHourlyFixedCost\) be the complete hourly fixed cost of the private deployment, including amortized hardware leases, provisioned failover headroom, and operational maintenance. Shared application costs cancel only when genuinely identical across both options, whereas differential tool, verification, network egress, or support fees must enter the balance sheet. Let \(\FeasibleOutputTokenRate\) denote the measured feasible output token generation rate (in tokens per second) under the target latency objective, a token rate distinct from the per-worker call rate \(\ServiceRate\) of the capacity model. Let \(\DeliveredCapacityFraction\) represent the fraction of that reference capacity the workload actually delivers. Unlike the queueing utilization \(\ServingUtilization\), which measures offered load against raw service capacity, \(\DeliveredCapacityFraction\) is measured against a rate already chosen to meet the latency target, so \(\DeliveredCapacityFraction=1\) still meets that target by construction. In one hour, the system produces \(3600\FeasibleOutputTokenRate \DeliveredCapacityFraction\) output tokens, costing \(3600\FeasibleOutputTokenRate \DeliveredCapacityFraction\,\EffectiveApiOutputTokenPrice\) via a pay-as-you-go API and \(\DedicatedHourlyFixedCost+3600\FeasibleOutputTokenRate \DeliveredCapacityFraction\,\DedicatedVariableTokenCost\) on dedicated hardware. When \(\EffectiveApiOutputTokenPrice>\DedicatedVariableTokenCost\), the two expenditure curves cross at the break-even utilization

\[\BreakEvenUtilization = \frac{\DedicatedHourlyFixedCost}{3600\FeasibleOutputTokenRate(\EffectiveApiOutputTokenPrice-\DedicatedVariableTokenCost)}. \tag{6}\]

Owned capacity wins only above the load that repays its fixed cost.

If \(\BreakEvenUtilization>1\), the dedicated deployment cannot break even within its physical capacity envelope; if \(\EffectiveApiOutputTokenPrice\le \DedicatedVariableTokenCost\) while \(\DedicatedHourlyFixedCost>0\), self-hosting remains permanently more expensive. Execution deadlines and concurrency limits must be verified separately at the target operating point.

Napkin Math 19.9: Pricing idle capacity and cached input
Problem: Assume a hypothetical matched-capability workload uses 5 input tokens per output token, with 80 percent of inputs billed at the cached rate. API prices per million tokens are $3 for uncached input, $0.3 for cached input, and $15 for output. A dedicated deployment costs $28 per hour for its provisioned capacity and operations, plus $2 per million output tokens for differential variable costs. Its feasible reference output rate is 1,200 tokens/s under the same mix, reserve policy, and latency target. Which alternative costs less at 52 percent of that rate?

Math: Weight the two input prices by their billed volume fractions, then incorporate associated input costs into \(\EffectiveApiOutputTokenPrice\). Apply equation 6. Both options are feasible at this operating point, and shared tool, evaluation, and application costs cancel.

Result: Weighted input costs $0.84 per million input tokens. Factoring in associated inputs, the API costs $19.2 per million output tokens. The cost crossing occurs at 37.7 percent of reference capacity. At the stated load, generating 2,246,400 output tokens per hour costs $43.13 through the API vs. $32.49 on dedicated capacity, saving $10.64 per hour.

Systems insight: The economic break-even threshold shifts directly with the API’s effective token price. Without cached input, the same workload would cross at 23.1 percent of reference capacity instead of 37.7 percent. Aggressive prefix caching by API providers erodes the economic incentive to own capacity. Teams should commit to dedicated capacity only when sustained production load reliably exceeds \(\BreakEvenUtilization\) with the headroom demanded by latency targets. Operating below the crossing wastes capital on idle silicon, while owning hardware places queueing congestion, node failures, and capacity management entirely on internal engineering teams.

Checkpoint 19.2: Check the capacity boundary

Before bounding what an individual trajectory may spend, evaluate the platform capacity model:

Capacity planning sizes the collective infrastructure required to sustain admitted workload streams. It says nothing about how much any one trajectory, or the tree of children it delegates to, may spend. That bound needs a different mechanism, a budget account that admits and settles every charge atomically.

19.7 Spending Governance

When a coordinator admits concurrent sub-agents or child tasks, simple balance checks collapse under race conditions. If two children inspect the remaining free balance simultaneously before either records a reservation, both admission checks succeed, yet their combined liability immediately breaches the authorized task budget. Task cancellation introduces a second concurrency bug: returning a stopped child’s allocation to the free pool while its remote service call remains billable allows another worker to spend funds that are already committed to third-party infrastructure. The harness already solved this for one trajectory. Its bounded-liability admission reserves an operation’s maximum charge before dispatch and releases it only at settlement (Reservation and settlement). Trajectory-level encapsulation (principle 10) requires the same invariant to hold when the allowance is split across children, and to stay conserved across concurrent dispatch, node crashes, and late settlement.

19.7.1 Liability bounds and admission

If an admission gate reserves only the average token cost of a model call, a single request that hits its maximum completion cap or misses the prompt cache will settle above its reservation and breach the task’s spending ceiling. To prevent overruns, let \(\BudgetSettledCharges(\BudgetEvaluationTime)\) denote gross charges settled into a task’s budget account at time \(\BudgetEvaluationTime\) and \(\BudgetReservedFunds(\BudgetEvaluationTime)\) the funds withheld there for outstanding liabilities, delegated allocations, and protected finalization. A task with authorized budget \(\CostCeiling\) maintains the invariant

\[\BudgetSettledCharges(\BudgetEvaluationTime)+\BudgetReservedFunds(\BudgetEvaluationTime)\le \CostCeiling.\]

The harness’s bounded-liability test (equation 10.1) enforces this invariant at admission. Before dispatch, the runtime reserves a strict upper bound \(\OperationChargeBound\) on the operation’s maximum charge across every billing category (for a model call, including the maximum completion and hidden reasoning tokens) rather than an expected cost. It funds teardown in advance. Under a hard-ceiling policy, it rejects or replaces any operation that lacks an enforceable bound.

Budget enforcement also depends on complete mediation, applied here to money: every billable interaction must pass through the accounting gateway. If worker nodes hold shared credentials that bypass the reservation gate, or if unmonitored retry loops dispatch billable requests off the books, the budget account’s guarantees no longer hold. Prompt caching and tiered volume discounts present a related operational subtlety: while cache hits reduce final settlement charges, they can reduce the upfront reservation \(\OperationChargeBound\) only if the provider contractually guarantees the discount prior to request dispatch. If a cache miss remains possible, the runtime must withhold the full un-discounted rate.

↩︎ Complete mediation, the requirement that every access pass through one checking point, is established for tool calls in Complete mediation.

The budget account tracks gross liabilities rather than net cash flows. Releasing an unspent reservation restores permission to spend money that was never actually charged. Subsequent provider credits, reconciliations, or post-hoc refunds are recorded as separate external adjustments that leave gross spending and historical authorizations intact. Fresh spending capacity enters the system only through an operator’s explicit budget grant, never through automatic replenishment from error recoveries or the speculative promise of a newly spawned candidate branch.

19.7.2 Budget conservation across descendants

If a fleet dashboard sums reserved balances across a parent and its children, it double-counts every delegated dollar; if it inspects only the parent’s settled charges, it misses active spending still accumulating in open child accounts. To keep balances conserved across a delegation tree, each node \(\BudgetNodeIndex\) maintains a runtime-owned budget account with the same structure, tracking its allocation \({\CostCeiling}_k\), settled gross charges \(\BudgetSettledCharges_k\), reservations \(\BudgetReservedFunds_k\), and free balance \(\BudgetFreeBalance_k\):

\[{\CostCeiling}_k=\BudgetSettledCharges_k+\BudgetReservedFunds_k+\BudgetFreeBalance_k,\qquad \BudgetSettledCharges_k,\BudgetReservedFunds_k,\BudgetFreeBalance_k\ge0.\]

A budget reservation, the harness’s bounded-liability reservation applied at node \(\BudgetNodeIndex\), removes funds from the free balance \(\BudgetFreeBalance_k\) before an operation can be dispatched. Multi-agent coordination partitions consumable budgets into nested sub-budgets. What this section adds is the settlement side, how a delegated allocation is counted, when it may be released, and what happens when a bill arrives late or exceeds its bound. In a hierarchical agent tree, delegating an allocation \(\DelegatedAllocationAmount\) to a child node withholds those funds in the parent’s reservation pool \(\ParentReservedFunds\) while the child manages them within its own budget account (\(\ChildBudgetAllocation = \ChildFreeBalance = \DelegatedAllocationAmount\)). The parent’s reservation and the child’s entire account thus represent the same backing funds seen from two levels of the tree. Fleet-wide reporting attributes each charge exactly once through its unique operation record, while the tree hierarchy tracks which branch holds the authority to back it.

The state transitions summarized in table 4 are strictly atomic with respect to competing admissions and control-plane changes. Because spending represents an irreversible external commitment, budget updates must durably survive the failure domain specified in Durable Intent Before Dispatch before any billable request leaves the runtime. If a worker node crashes, a replacement coordinator reconstructs active reservations directly from this write-ahead log rather than resetting balances because the former worker disappeared. As in multi-agent delegation, budget differs from permission because it is consumed. A child’s read grant leaves the parent’s read access intact, whereas a delegated allocation withholds funds the parent could otherwise spend, which is why the budget account must track it as a reservation.

↩︎ Shared permissions, partitioned consumable budgets, and nested sub-budgets under delegation are established in Attenuated grants and split allowances.

Every budget reservation is tied to a unique compound identifier binding the task, logical operation, transport attempt number, pricing tier, and cost bound \(\OperationChargeBound\). When downstream work finishes, settlement requires authenticated usage receipts evaluated against a durable settlement identity, the idempotency key under which the final charge is recorded in the budget account. It is settlement before retry (principle 8) applied to money. The operation and attempt identifiers that let a tool call be retried without duplicating its effect also let a bill be redelivered without duplicating its charge. If network retries cause the provider to deliver identical billing evidence multiple times, the runtime applies the state transition at most once, verifying that repeated deliveries contain identical payloads. A conflicting payload under an existing settlement identity indicates a reconciliation fault, which the runtime must neither blindly overwrite nor silently ignore. When long-running services report intermediate consumption, the runtime can incrementally move settled amounts into \(\BudgetSettledCharges_k\) while continuing to withhold a bound on remaining liabilities. It releases the reservation entirely only upon definitive finality. Deduplicating a remote side effect at an API endpoint and deduplicating an internal budget record serve two distinct contracts.

↩︎ Operation and attempt identifiers, which let a retried tool call be recognized as the same logical operation, are established in Operation identity and endpoint contracts.

If an external service reports a charge that exceeds its initial reservation (\(\SettledOperationCharge > \OperationChargeBound\)), the system’s foundational cost invariant has failed. Silently clamping the reported charge to \(\OperationChargeBound\) or allowing the free balance \(\BudgetFreeBalance_k\) to drop below zero would conceal a critical safety failure. Instead, the runtime immediately flags the discrepancy, halts further admissions within the affected subtree, and escalates the incident for reconciliation. Until an administrator or reconciliation procedure resolves the dispute, the system’s strict financial guarantee is temporarily suspended. Provided cost bounds remain valid, admissions execute atomically, and settlements are strictly deduplicated, every state transition preserves non-negative balances (\(\BudgetSettledCharges_k, \BudgetReservedFunds_k, \BudgetFreeBalance_k \ge 0\)) and guarantees that no child sub-agent can spend outside its allocated backing.

Cancellation is where conservation is most easily broken. The cancellation context tree of Cancellation context trees stops a child from admitting new billable work the moment control is revoked, but remote requests, inference batches, and cloud jobs the child already dispatched may keep running and billing until their own timeouts fire. The conservation rule therefore treats a stopped child as still open, and its whole allocation stays in the parent’s reservation until every outstanding operation reaches authenticated final settlement (the stop row of table 4). A runtime that does not want to keep an inactive child’s account in memory may hand it to a settlement coordinator. This is allowed only if the handover carries every known charge, outstanding reservation, and unencumbered balance with its operation lineage, so that at no point can funds be released twice or a liability be left unbacked. The finalization reserve of the harness’s admission test, applied at each node, funds the status queries and sandbox teardown that settlement requires after ordinary admission has closed, without reopening general spending authority. Accounting reports that cross a cutoff boundary distinguish settled historical charges from active liabilities held in reserve. The notebook below traces this distinction for a canceled child that receives a late bill from an external provider.

Napkin Math 19.10: A canceled child with a late bill
Problem: A hypothetical repair has a $5 budget. Its planning charge has settled at $0.042, and it allocates $1.20 to trace analysis and $2.50 to fuzzing. The trace child closes with a final charge of $0.38. The fuzzing child has settled $0.85 and holds $1 for a remote operation when its local executor confirms termination. No further child work is admitted. The remote operation later terminates with a final additional bill of $0.70. All other charges and required finalization are included in these records, and neither child has descendants. When can the root release the fuzzing allocation?

Math: The entire child allocation remains reserved at the root until its account closes. Table 5 follows the root’s balances under the same conservation rule throughout.

At local termination, the fuzzing child’s account has $0.65 free, but its remote reservation remains outstanding. Final settlement releases the unused part of that reservation, leaving $0.95 to return when the child closes. Re-delivery of the same final billing record changes none of these balances.

Systems insight: A premature release upon local process termination would have returned $1.65 (the allocation minus already-settled charges), even though only $0.65 was truly unencumbered. The subsequent $0.70 bill would then have landed on funds already committed to another child task. The governing invariant upon cancellation is strict: release at most the allocation minus settled charges minus the enforceable upper bound on outstanding work, and return the remainder only as each remote operation reaches final settlement. Financial safety is only as reliable as those upper bounds and the completeness of the budget records.

The conservation rule shows up visually as bars of equal length (figure 5). At every boundary of table 5, funds move among settled, reserved, and free balances without leaving the root’s budget, and the root’s reservation stays fixed from the fuzzing child’s local stop until its late bill settles.

19.7.3 Spending alarms and admission limits

Even when a cumulative task budget is strictly conserved, an overall ceiling cannot prevent damaging expenditure spikes. If an unconstrained agent launches dozens of model calls concurrently, it can burn through its entire allocation in seconds. A retrospective spending alarm monitors the burn rate by dividing the gross charges settled over a trailing window of duration \(\SpendingWindowDuration\) by \(\SpendingWindowDuration\). However, because external billing is often delayed, a retrospective alarm can stay quiet while expensive requests are in flight and spike only after execution has already halted. While retrospective alarms are useful for triggering administrative alerts or throttling subsequent work, they act after the fact, on money that has already been spent.

To prevent rapid budget exhaustion, the runtime must enforce proactive admission limits that evaluate new financial commitments, in the form of reservations, before dispatch. For a spending rate limit permitting at most \(\WindowSpendingLimit\) in new liabilities over any trailing window of duration \(\SpendingWindowDuration\), a robust admission policy enforces

\[\sum_{i:\,t-\SpendingWindowDuration<t_i\le t}\OperationChargeBound_i\le \WindowSpendingLimit,\]

where \(\TimeInstant_i\) is the admission timestamp and \(\OperationChargeBound_i\) is the bounded charge of each billable operation.9 Every prospective admission atomically validates both this sliding-window limit and the cumulative budget balance. This window constraint applies across all child tasks within the coordinator’s scope, rather than granting a fresh window allowance to every newly spawned worker. Delegating an allocation down the agent tree is an internal balance partition, not a newly admitted billable dispatch. Furthermore, retaining \(\OperationChargeBound_i\) in the window calculation until its admission timestamp ages out prevents a rapid sequence of fast-completing calls from triggering an unbounded burst. This policy guarantees a strict upper bound on committed work over the interval, regardless of when external providers finish processing requests or transmit final invoices.

9 Trailing Window vs. Token Bucket: Standard network and API rate limiters often employ token buckets, which permit an initial burst equal to the bucket capacity followed by a steady refill rate; over a short interval \(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant\), maximum consumption can reach capacity plus rate times \(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant\). In contrast, the trailing-window sum strictly caps committed liabilities across every continuous window of duration \(\SpendingWindowDuration\) at \(\WindowSpendingLimit\), irrespective of burst shape, at the modest implementation cost of retaining timestamped admission records until they age out of the window.

Rate-limiting policies must also protect capacity and budget for mandatory finalization routines. If an aggressive rate limiter blocks all outgoing traffic during a spending spike, it can starve the very cancellation and status queries required to terminate running tasks and settle outstanding reservations. Dedicating isolated execution lanes or explicit finalization allowances within the accounting policy prevents this deadlock. Furthermore, completing a diagnostic probe or clearing an operational alarm must never prematurely release funds held for unrelated active reservations.

When an agent enters a non-converging loop, evidenced by repeated execution failures or unvarying candidate artifacts, the runtime triggers a spending hold under its semantic watchdog policies. This intervention blocks new ordinary billable work before runaway iterations exhaust the task budget. Already-dispatched work may continue accruing charges within its reserved bounds. The runtime enforces the designated cancellation or escalation workflow while keeping all outstanding liabilities reserved. Evaluating whether completed work meets task requirements remains the responsibility of the domain acceptance contract, rather than the spending controller.

↩︎ Semantic watchdogs, which detect a non-converging loop from its progress evidence and place the trajectory in an admission hold, are established in Semantic Watchdogs.

Budget reservations turn spending governance from after-the-fact telemetry into an enforceable contract under declared pricing and failure models. They decide what a design may spend, not whether it is worth spending on. With cost, latency, and residual liabilities bounded, the remaining choice is which design delivers enough accepted work for its expense: a deterministic workflow, a single foundation model invocation, a bounded loop, or a delegated tree of agents.

19.8 Pricing the Choice of Architecture

A spending bound makes a proposed architecture admissible under its budget, but cost per accepted task determines whether it is worth deploying. When choosing among deterministic code, a fixed model-assisted workflow, a bounded loop, and multi-agent delegation, system architects often frame the decision as an ideological debate between programmatic control and model autonomy. In production systems engineering, however, this decision is an empirical optimization problem governed by an acceptance contract. The engineering objective comes first, because minimizing expected financial cost subject to a completion requirement selects a different architecture than minimizing tail latency or deadline misses under a spending ceiling.

19.8.1 Design alternatives

A fixed workflow that unconditionally calls a frontier model on every ticket can cost more than an adaptive, bounded loop that resolves simple cases on turn one with a lightweight model. A single model call proposing a database deletion likewise requires the same authorization checks as a ten-turn agent, and parallel delegated trajectories can cut wall-clock time while inflating total token consumption. Architectural alternatives differ primarily in who selects the next operation, as table 6 summarizes, yet control authority alone settles neither execution cost, permitted environmental side effects, nor end-to-end latency.

↩︎ Foundational trade-offs between fixed workflows and model-directed loops are established in Workflows vs. model-directed loops.

Every design can carry state, require crash recovery, and demand substantive verification. A rigorous comparison therefore holds the acceptance contract strictly constant across candidate designs. Verification is neither relaxed for a single model call nor tightened for an agentic loop or a delegated system.

19.8.2 Task obligations

Whether a dependency upgrade runs as a deterministic script or a multi-agent tree, it still has to pass the regression suite, retain workspace state across retries, and hold authorization before merging. The H·S·A exposures that have organized this book, together with the closure evidence the contract demands, do not dictate an architecture. They name the obligations each candidate design must pay for:

  • Horizon: A longer execution horizon expands the trajectory space, multiplying opportunities for resource consumption, transient network faults, and compounding drift. In cost accounting, these costs are priced not by hypothetical maximum depth, but by the expected distribution of steps that the runtime policy actually reaches.
  • State: State incurs retrieval overhead, storage retention, consistency maintenance, and checkpoint recovery mechanisms wherever it resides. An adaptive loop justifies this stateful overhead only when fresh observations yield updates that substantially improve downstream decisions, generating enough value to repay their operational cost.
  • Authority: The scope of authority determines which operations demand formal admission, evidence collection, and two-phase settlement. Irreversible actions, such as modifying financial records, sending customer emails, or deleting cloud infrastructure, require an explicit authorization and risk policy, often incorporating cryptographic audit trails or human approval gates. Any unresolved effect or outstanding external reservation remains an open liability on the account, regardless of the expected inference bill.
  • Closure evidence: Closure evidence governs what repeated attempts can actually establish. An automated, executable check, such as a compiler, test suite, or schema validator, supplies fast, inexpensive, and highly specific feedback. The economic value of this feedback depends directly on the test harness coverage and its false-acceptance rate. When no automated check exists, an agent must seek closure through additional observations, queries to independent data sources, or escalation to human review. Human review, however, introduces its own substantial hourly costs, queueing delays, and error rates. Routing an ambiguous task to a person does not count as completing it. The task counts as completed only when the human reviewer finishes the work and fulfills the acceptance contract.

19.8.3 When a bounded loop beats a single call

Pricing those obligations on the dependency-upgrade service shows when a multi-turn loop earns its keep over simpler designs. The engineering team can deploy a deterministic rule-based rewriter, a single frontier model call, or a smaller, cheaper model that retries for up to three turns within a bounded loop before escalating failed tickets to a human engineer. The deterministic rewriter and the single model call each have a single static acceptance rate, whereas the loop’s overall acceptance rate and aggregate spend depend on the exact distribution of turns it reaches before stopping.

For a bounded loop, let \(\StageConditionalAcceptanceProbability_t\) denote the probability that turn \(\TurnIndex\) produces an accepted candidate, conditional on reaching turn \(\TurnIndex\) after rejections on all preceding turns. Assuming that every reached turn undergoes a complete acceptance assessment, that execution halts immediately upon acceptance, and that the runtime enforces a hard budget of \(\TrajectoryTurnLimit\) turns, the cumulative automated acceptance probability is:

\[\LoopAcceptanceProbability(\TrajectoryTurnLimit) = 1 - \prod_{t=1}^{\TrajectoryTurnLimit} (1 - \StageConditionalAcceptanceProbability_t) \tag{7}\]

As with model cascading, this formulation applies the probability chain rule rather than assuming independence across turns. The system reaches turn \(\TurnIndex\) with probability \(\StageReachProbability_t=\prod_{j<t}(1-\StageConditionalAcceptanceProbability_j)\), where \(\StageReachProbability_1=1\). If \(\LoopTurnCost_t\) represents the comprehensive cost of turn \(\TurnIndex\) (model inference, tool execution, sandbox lease duration, and the assessment check), the expected automated cost per incoming ticket is \(\sum_{t=1}^{\TrajectoryTurnLimit} \StageReachProbability_t \LoopTurnCost_t\). When turns incur an identical per-turn cost \(\UniformLoopTurnCost\), this expectation simplifies to \(\UniformLoopTurnCost \sum_{t=1}^{\TrajectoryTurnLimit} \StageReachProbability_t\), where \(\sum_{t=1}^{\TrajectoryTurnLimit} \StageReachProbability_t\) is the expected number of executed turns. Operational exceptions, such as test harness outages or runtime abandonment triggered by safety filters, introduce distinct terminal states that must be accounted for as separate branches rather than conflated with normal test failures.

Accounting becomes more complex when human escalation enters the operational policy. Suppose an automated tier achieves acceptance probability \(\AttemptPassProbability\) at expected cost \(\MeanAttemptCost\), and routes every rejected ticket to a human fallback workflow that costs \(\HumanFallbackCost\) and achieves conditional resolution probability \(\HumanResolutionProbability\). The expected total expenditure per incoming ticket is \(\MeanAttemptCost+(1-\AttemptPassProbability)\HumanFallbackCost\), while the combined resolution probability across automated and human tiers is \(\AttemptPassProbability+(1-\AttemptPassProbability)\HumanResolutionProbability\). Dividing expected spend by combined resolution yields the true cost per resolved ticket under the end-to-end policy: \[\frac{\MeanAttemptCost + (1 - \AttemptPassProbability) \HumanFallbackCost}{\AttemptPassProbability + (1 - \AttemptPassProbability) \HumanResolutionProbability}\] In contrast, the isolated metric \(\MeanAttemptCost/\AttemptPassProbability\) captures only the cost per automated acceptance. Conflating these two metrics obscures the true cost structure of the system, particularly when automated acceptance is low. Neither ratio is well-defined when its respective denominator reaches zero. The same account sets a bounded loop’s cap. When the fallback resolves every ticket it receives (\(\HumanResolutionProbability=1\)), the combined resolution probability in the denominator is fixed at one regardless of how many turns the loop runs, so adding turn \(\TurnIndex\) changes only the numerator. A ticket reaches turn \(\TurnIndex\) with probability \(\StageReachProbability_t\) after failing all earlier turns. Stopping before turn \(\TurnIndex\) sends that entire \(\StageReachProbability_t\) remainder to the human fallback, whereas running turn \(\TurnIndex\) pays \(\StageReachProbability_t\LoopTurnCost_t\) to resolve a fraction \(\StageConditionalAcceptanceProbability_t\) of that remainder automatically and avoids \(\StageReachProbability_t\StageConditionalAcceptanceProbability_t\HumanFallbackCost\) of fallback spend. Because \(\StageReachProbability_t\) multiplies both terms, it cancels out, and extending the cap to turn \(\TurnIndex\) lowers expected spend per resolved ticket whenever \(\StageConditionalAcceptanceProbability_t\HumanFallbackCost>\LoopTurnCost_t\). When the human fallback is imperfect (\(\HumanResolutionProbability<1\)), this simple comparison no longer suffices because an extra automated turn also raises the combined resolution probability in the denominator. Even at \(\HumanResolutionProbability=1\), this adjacent-cap comparison assumes immediate fallback and unchanged earlier turns rather than serving as a general stopping rule for policies with further contingent choices. The executable model in notebook 19.11 illustrates why keeping these two metrics separate is essential for sound architectural selection.

Napkin Math 19.11: Pricing three designs for dependency upgrades
Problem: A software engineering team manages an influx of 10,000 dependency-upgrade pull requests each month. Every upgrade requires code modifications that must pass an identical build and regression test suite to be accepted. The team considers three architectural options:

  1. A deterministic rule-based rewriter that costs $0.001 per attempt before verification and achieves automated acceptance on 32 percent of tickets.
  2. A single frontier model call that costs $0.045 before verification and accepts 68 percent of tickets.
  3. A bounded loop powered by a lightweight model that costs $0.006 per reached turn before verification. This loop halts immediately upon acceptance or caps execution at three turns, with conditional acceptance probabilities of 0.55 on turn 1, 0.42 on turn 2, and 0.30 on turn 3.

Every candidate generated by any automated tier incurs an identical $0.002 charge for build and regression testing. Automated costs comprehensively include model inference, process execution, sandbox container reservation, and workspace teardown. Any ticket rejected by automation escalates to a human engineer; human fallback incurs $35 per ticket (including manual triage, debugging, and final verification sign-off) and resolves 90 percent of escalated tickets. Under these population expectations, which architecture minimizes the total cost per resolved ticket while satisfying a mandatory service floor of 95 percent combined resolution?

Math: Applying equation 7, the bounded loop achieves an automated acceptance rate of 81.73 percent while executing 1.711 turns on average per ticket. The cost breakdown in table 7 separates automated acceptance from combined resolution, preventing expensive human resolutions from masquerading as automated successes.

Systems insight: When human escalation follows automated rejection, fallback labor dominates the economics of the system. Because human handling costs $35, each percentage point of automated acceptance saves $0.35 in expected fallback expenditure per incoming ticket, several times the entire automated spend of any candidate architecture. Although the deterministic rewriter looks attractive under isolated automated accounting ($0.00937 per automated acceptance), it yields the highest combined cost ($25.54 per resolved ticket) and fails to satisfy the 95 percent resolution floor. The bounded loop wins ($6.53 per resolved ticket) because its second and third retry turns successfully salvage rejections that would otherwise trigger human intervention. Across 10,000 monthly tickets, the loop delivers an expected total spend of $64,081.88 for 9,817.3 successfully resolved upgrades. This ranking directly reflects the conditional acceptance yield of subsequent turns relative to human triage costs, relationships that engineering teams must empirically benchmark in production.

This steady-state model assumes that all verification jobs complete cleanly and that all resource charges settle immediately. In a live production evaluation, however, telemetry systems must track in-flight runs, timeout aborts, orphaned sandbox containers, and pending verifier verdicts at the observation cutoff, charging every initiated attempt to the cost total. Human fallback must be evaluated against the exact same task contract as automation. Human reviewers must pass the identical build and regression suite rather than being granted informal operational waivers.

Furthermore, expected cost per resolved ticket does not tell the whole operational story. The model leaves queueing dynamics, completion-time distributions, and human engineering bandwidth unmodeled. If incoming tickets arrive in sudden bursts, or if the engineering staff has fixed capacity, routing even a small fraction of failures to human triage can saturate the queue and introduce long review delays. Under stringent service-level agreements or strict staffing limits, a team may deliberately select a direct frontier model call over a bounded loop or rewriter, even at higher automated spend, when measured acceptance is higher and fewer tickets escape to human queues. In the dependency-upgrade scenario, the bounded loop has higher automated acceptance than the single call, so it also sends fewer tickets to human fallback.

Multi-agent delegation obeys this same objective-first economic evaluation when benchmarked against a capable single controller. Decomposing a complex task across a coordinator and specialized child agents pays the coordination tax (principle 14) in two places. Handoffs, context delivery, and integration on the critical path enter equation 18.1 as the overhead term that holds any speedup below its serial-fraction ceiling, the same form of ceiling that capped faster inference at \(1/(1-\ModelTimeFraction)\) in notebook 19.5. Token amplification and child work off the critical path enter the numerator of cost per accepted task whether or not they shorten the task. Whether a given workload repays that premium, by meeting a deadline a sequential loop would miss or by higher conditional acceptance on subproblems, is the matched delegation decision of A complete delegation decision. In this accounting, delegation is one more architecture priced under the same contract. Economic viability does not require an architecture to dominate every competing design on both cost and latency simultaneously. Pareto dominance is sufficient, but in real systems engineering, meeting a critical operational deadline under an admissible budget is what decides the deployment.

19.8.4 Payback of an adaptation investment

The comparisons so far priced only per-task operating spend, but replacing a general model with a smaller fine-tuned one also carries a one-time engineering bill that must be recovered before the switch saves money. Let \(\AdaptationInvestmentCost\) denote that one-time cost of dataset curation, training compute, and offline evaluation, and let \(\BaselineTaskCost\) and \(\AdaptedTaskCost\) be the expected operating costs per assigned task under the baseline and adapted configurations, including failed attempts, retries, and downstream human review or recovery. Assuming stationary unit costs over the planning horizon, a positive per-task saving \(\FiniteChange \TaskCost = \BaselineTaskCost - \AdaptedTaskCost\) yields the continuous break-even threshold:

\[\BreakEvenTaskCount = \frac{\AdaptationInvestmentCost}{\BaselineTaskCost - \AdaptedTaskCost} = \frac{\AdaptationInvestmentCost}{\FiniteChange \TaskCost}, \qquad \FiniteChange \TaskCost > 0. \tag{8}\]

The relation is the payback horizon of context compaction (section 19.3.2) moved up one scale. There a one-time summarization surcharge is recovered over the remaining calls of one trajectory; here a one-time engineering investment is recovered over the tasks of a deployment. Because tasks arrive in whole units, the investment is recovered at \(\lceil \BreakEvenTaskCount \rceil\) tasks. If the per-task saving is nonpositive (\(\FiniteChange \TaskCost \le 0\)), the investment never breaks even on operating cost, and a more expensive configuration must be justified by quality, latency tails, a required capability, or reduced risk. If the two configurations accept different fractions of tasks, cost per assigned task misleads, and the comparison must use cost per accepted task (equation 1.2).

Napkin Math 19.12: Conditional payback of a smaller adapted model
Scenario: A cheaper model can repay its adaptation only where model tokens are a large share of each attempt, which rules out workloads whose cost is dominated by human approval or sandbox holding. Consider instead a short, high-volume workload whose token bill a smaller model does change. An engineering team is evaluating a smaller fine-tuned model for automated code review. Both configurations must meet identical acceptance criteria and latency bounds with equivalent acceptance rates, which offline and shadow evaluation must establish before any cost model can justify a rollout. Step and token figures are averages across all assigned reviews, including failed attempts and retries.

Variables: The generalist baseline consumes 12,000 billable input tokens and 800 billable output tokens per step across an average of 5 steps. Inference pricing stands at $3 per million input tokens and $15 per million output tokens. In contrast, the smaller adapted model operates with a distilled prompt consuming 1,500 input tokens and 800 output tokens across 4 steps, billed at $1 and $4 per million tokens, respectively. These figures assume standard on-demand token billing without prompt-caching discounts.

The upfront engineering investment entails $4,000 for data collection and curation, $500 for training compute, and $8,000 for rigorous evaluation and benchmark verification, totaling an incremental expenditure of $12,500. The expected deployment workload is 20,000 assigned reviews per month. All ancillary costs (developer tooling, human escalation, and infrastructure maintenance) are assumed equivalent across configurations and cancel out of the marginal equation.

Math: Executing a single step under the baseline policy costs 12,000 × $3 plus 800 × $15 per million tokens, yielding $0.048 per step and an expected task cost of $0.2400. A step under the adapted policy costs $0.0047, totaling $0.0188 per task. Under stationary cost assumptions, the intervention produces an incremental operational saving of $0.2212 per assigned task. Applying equation 8 yields a continuous break-even threshold of approximately 56,509.95 tasks. The discrete integer crossing occurs at 56,510 tasks. At steady monthly volume, this investment pays for itself in approximately 2.83 months.

Systems insight: Because break-even duration equals \(\BreakEvenTaskCount\) divided by monthly volume, the economic horizon is governed by deployment volume and verification overhead rather than raw training compute. Evaluation accounts for 64 percent of the initial investment, whereas training compute represents just 4 percent. On the operational side, reduced input tokens generate 79 percent of the net per-task savings, so the case depends on the adapted model staying competent under a much shorter prompt. If traffic drops to a quarter of anticipated volume (5,000 reviews per month), the payback timeline stretches to 11.3 months. These savings apply per assigned task. They translate to cost per accepted task only as long as end-to-end acceptance rates remain equal (equation 1.2). If the adapted model’s acceptance falls, the comparison must be rerun on cost per accepted task. The human escalation and review costs assumed above to cancel no longer cancel, because each rejected review now creates human work the baseline did not.

An adaptation is therefore priced like any other architecture change on the task account, with its one-time engineering cost spread over the volume it serves and its operating saving conditional on unchanged acceptance. The dependency-upgrade and code-review comparisons each priced candidate designs for a single focused task; stepping back to diverse production workloads shows how the same account behaves when different cost terms dominate the numerator.

19.9 Cost per Accepted Task by Archetype

Even within the model-inference bill alone, token volume and dollar spend diverge sharply across production deployments. On the multi-provider gateway introduced in The Evolution of Machine Learning Systems, open-weight models serve 71.5 percent of all tokens while accounting for 22.6 percent of inference expenditure, whereas closed-weight frontier models carry the remaining 28.5 percent of tokens and 77.4 percent of spend (8.6× higher average spend per token). Figure 6 shows how that tier split separates token volume from dollar spend across eight workload classes on the same gateway.

High-volume personal assistants route routine turns to inexpensive tiers, consuming 40 percent of gateway tokens for 19.6 percent of total spend (0.49× the gateway average per token). Coding agents balance exploration on fast tiers against patch synthesis on stronger models (21.8 percent of tokens and 20.4 percent of spend). Back-office automation concentrates on premium reasoning models, drawing 15 percent of spend from 5.8 percent of tokens (2.59× the gateway average, or 5.3× as much per token as personal assistants).

Yet the gateway invoice captures only the model-token term of an attempt. Widening the boundary to the whole-trajectory account across the book’s four lighthouse workloads shows how those token bills interact with sandbox residency, external tool fees, verification, and human review. The software repair agent holds a sandbox and an expanding workspace context over long multi-turn trajectories, whereas the research agent fans out parallel requests across untrusted external sources. The remediation agent exercises high-privilege authority over live production infrastructure under human approval, and the customer support agent processes high volumes of brief sessions against tight latency targets. Because a different cost term dominates each of these four archetypes, each workload responds to a different optimization lever.

Lighthouse 19.1: Cost per accepted task by archetype
Each lighthouse workload executes an attempt under its specific acceptance contract, priced in table 8 using the cost framework developed throughout this chapter. For the repair agent, the execution horizon reflects the step limit of the registry’s reference coding harness, with token costs derived from the prefix-cached, append-only trajectory in notebook 19.2. This repair row is a scenario of its own, independent of the opening cohort of notebook 19.1, whose short attempts and acceptance counts were chosen only to isolate the token-price effect. For the research agent, model costs scale according to the 15× token fan-out multiple reported for a production multi-agent research system (Hadfield et al. 2025). The multiple is treated here as one measured baseline rather than a universal constant, and it is applied to a standard conversational turn at the chapter’s base tariff. For the remediation agent, human costs reflect an on-call engineer reviewing and approving a high-stakes production patch. For the support agent, every failed or rejected session triggers an immediate escalation handoff to a human representative at $4. The support account counts automated accepted sessions in its denominator and includes handoff setup for rejected sessions in its numerator. Subsequent human resolution, its costs, and its completion delays lie outside this account. A combined service must add those costs and count verified human completions separately, as in section 19.8.3. All remaining inputs represent baseline scenario values.

Systems insight: The economic trade-off between cheaper inference and higher task accuracy follows a direct mathematical relationship. Halving the unit token price saves half of the model’s share of the attempt. In contrast, raising the acceptance probability from \(\AttemptPassProbability\) to \(\AttemptPassProbability+\FiniteChange \AttemptPassProbability\) saves the fraction \(\FiniteChange \AttemptPassProbability/(\AttemptPassProbability+\FiniteChange \AttemptPassProbability)\) across all fixed per-attempt cost components in the repair, research, and remediation rows. Support also saves handoff setup as fewer sessions are rejected. Under these held-cost assumptions, higher acceptance gives the greater reduction whenever the model’s share of total attempt cost falls below \(2\FiniteChange \AttemptPassProbability/(\AttemptPassProbability+\FiniteChange \AttemptPassProbability)\).

For the software repair agent, this decision threshold sits at 40 percent. Because model tokens account for only 33.2 percent of the repair agent’s attempt cost, improving acceptance by 10 percentage points saves more money than cutting token tariffs in half under those assumptions. Selecting an intervention also requires pricing the work and latency that produce the acceptance improvement. In this account, the repair agent is economically bound by holding its sandbox and tools across 30 turns. That is non-model work again, much as tests and sandbox lifecycle rather than model calls dominated elapsed time in notebook 19.5, although here the binding term is residency cost rather than test time.

Each archetype exposes a different binding constraint. Only the research agent is bound by token expenditure, with model calls consuming 85.7 percent of its attempt cost due to combinatorial sub-agent fan-out. Prefix caching, context compaction, and bounded search horizons are its primary economic levers. The remediation agent is dominated by senior engineer approval time. The approval escrow of Held proposals makes each wait cheap to hold but does not shorten it. Systems architects shorten review by binding each approval to one exact configuration version and by grouping several reversible actions under one approval, balanced against the expected penalty of irreversible failures. Finally, the customer support agent is dominated by escalation handoffs. Because every failed session incurs an escalation fee, higher acceptance both raises the denominator and removes human labor from the numerator. Because the support agent’s cost per accepted task is well under a dollar, its interactive latency objective still requires separate fleet-capacity checks using the queueing models of section 19.6. A small financial ratio alone does not establish fleet saturation.

Hadfield, Jeremy, Barry Zhang, Kenneth Lien, Florian Scholz, Jeremy Fox, and Daniel Ford. 2025. How We Built Our Multi-Agent Research System. Anthropic Engineering Blog.

When transitioning from architectural design evaluations to continuous production monitoring, an engineering team must maintain consistent accounting boundaries within its operational reporting. Complete cost accounting must track aborted and canceled attempts, idle headroom in shared hosting pools, human review and escalation labor within the task contract, and unresolved liabilities at the accounting cutoff. Preserving this rigorous boundary allows teams to re-evaluate architectural rankings as operating conditions evolve. When an inference provider slashes model pricing, a product release shifts the difficulty distribution of incoming tasks, or an organization updates its safety and approval policies, the matched economic comparison must be re-run. A design that appears cheaper on paper should only be promoted to production after its sandbox environments, test runners, and reviewer pools have been validated under realistic peak admission rates.

19.10 Fallacies and Pitfalls

An economic evaluation can be mathematically precise yet misleading if it tracks the wrong unit of work or quietly shifts the standard of acceptance. In agent systems, these errors typically occur when localized metrics, such as a model’s raw token tariff, an isolated inference speedup, or a child agent’s premature stopping condition, substitute for the end-to-end cost of completed, accepted tasks.

Fallacy: The lowest token price identifies the cheapest accepted task.

Selecting a model based solely on published input and output token rates ignores the operational overhead of lower model capability. Cheaper models often require longer prompts, take additional interactive turns, invoke tools redundantly, and fail validation gates more frequently. Because every rejected attempt still consumes compute, environment time, and assessment overhead, a low success rate rapidly erodes nominal token savings.

As demonstrated in notebook 19.1, switching to a lower-priced model cuts nominal token charges by 71.3 percent and reduces per-attempt cost by 19.8 percent. However, because its completion rate drops, the system costs 2.4× as much per accepted repair. Factoring in the manual triage required for rejected attempts widens the gap further, driving effective costs to 8.1× the baseline. To measure real economics, systems engineers must evaluate the total cost of all initiated work against the count of accepted solutions delivered under an identical task contract (section 19.2).

Pitfall: Buying faster generation without identifying what delays completion.

Accelerating token generation shortens only decode spans. Accelerating complete model calls is a different hypothetical intervention that can also shorten input processing. When an agent spends most of its trajectory compiling code, executing sandbox tests, or waiting on remote APIs, Amdahl’s law strictly limits the returns of a faster model.

Consider the serial software repair episode evaluated in notebook 19.5. Applying a 4× model speedup trims 21.94 s from a 312.40 s trajectory, an overall gain of only 1.08×. Even if complete model calls were instantaneous, the trajectory speedup would cap out at 1.10×. By comparison, halving the test execution interval saves 122.8 s. Engineers should map the end-to-end critical path across non-overlapping execution phases, tool interactions, and queueing delays before investing in premium low-latency inference (section 19.4).

Fallacy: Prefix caching makes carried context nearly free.

While prompt prefix caching sharply discounts the cost of reading previously processed tokens, it does not alter the quadratic volume growth inherent to multi-turn interactions. As an agent’s context window expands turn by turn, the append-only input inference bill retains a quadratic growth term whenever the cached-read tariff is positive, even at heavily discounted rates. This need not dominate the complete operational account.

The analysis in notebook 19.2 shows that ideal cache reuse cuts the token bill by 5.6×. Nevertheless, re-reading cached history still constitutes 50.8 percent of the remaining token bill, growing with the square of the trajectory horizon. Moreover, these savings remain fragile, since a modest drop of 10 percent in eligible cache hits raises the cached bill by 45.7 percent. Sustained efficiency requires strict prefix stability across requests. It also requires compacting conversational state only when the remaining horizon repays the rebuilding cost or context limits justify it, while preserving task acceptance (section 19.3).

Pitfall: Running shared model capacity near saturation to lower its unit cost.

Maximizing cluster utilization to amortize server hardware yields diminishing economic returns once queueing effects take hold. While throughput scales linearly with load, queue wait times surge hyperbolically as utilization approaches saturation. For multi-turn agents, this latency penalty does not occur in isolation. It compounds sequentially across every turn in the trajectory.

In notebook 19.8, driving utilization from 70 percent to 90 percent delivers 28.6 percent more throughput, but it multiplies the mean queue wait by 3.9×, and a serial trajectory pays that longer wait on every step. Under the independent per-call tail model, a run of 30 serial calls meets at least one call slower than its own 99th percentile with probability 0.26. This probability does not specify a trajectory deadline-miss rate. Near saturation, the single-server heavy-traffic approximation also predicts longer queue-delay percentiles. Correlated delays and shared serving pools require their own trajectory-level measurements. Infrastructure engineers should provision reserve headroom to satisfy end-to-end trajectory percentiles rather than optimizing for average single-call server utilization (section 19.6).

Fallacy: A cascade’s cheap first tier makes its savings independent of its gate.

Routing tasks to a cheaper model cascade yields net savings only if the validation gate reliably catches defective outputs. When an inexpensive early tier falsely accepts an incorrect result, the downstream cost of detecting and repairing that defect in production quickly outweighs any upstream token savings.

In notebook 19.6, the three-tier cascade saves $0.053 per accepted task over direct frontier model execution. This saving assumes that early exits satisfy the exact same acceptance contract as escalated runs. If a relaxed tier-1 gate falsely accepts only 0.9 percent of its exits, and each defective output costs $10 to remediate, the resulting harm wipes out the cascade’s financial advantage entirely. Robust cascade design requires pricing gate evaluation, escalation overhead, and downstream failure remediation together under a unified quality contract (section 19.5).

Pitfall: Returning a canceled child’s unused-looking allowance to the spending pool at local stop.

In distributed multi-agent systems, local process termination does not guarantee remote financial settlement. When a coordinator terminates or cancels a child agent, asynchronous API calls and external tool invocations often remain in flight. Reallocating an agent’s unspent budget back to the common pool the moment it halts locally creates a race condition against incoming delayed charges.

In notebook 19.10, releasing funds immediately upon local termination appears to free $1.65, whereas only $0.65 is truly uncommitted. When an in-flight tool run posts a late charge of $0.70, it overdraws funds already promised to a sibling task. Budget coordinators must calculate released funds as the initial allocation minus settled charges minus strict, enforceable upper bounds on outstanding work. Reliable governance depends on atomic budget reservations and idempotent settlement, which prevent concurrent sibling agents from double-spending unsettled allowances. Retrospective spend alerts cannot prevent these overages (section 19.7).

19.11 Summary

An agent service is judged and funded by its accepted outcomes rather than its raw token consumption. Every operational expense the runtime incurs, on successful and failed attempts alike, is amortized across the cohort of accepted tasks under a given service contract. The central economic objective is therefore the effective cost per accepted task, \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\), evaluated across a settled cohort while explicitly accounting for cutoff thresholds and in-flight work. Any proposed optimization earns its keep only if it lowers \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) or meets a latency deadline that the system objective values. Evaluating such changes requires a strictly matched comparison that holds the task contract constant while the underlying policy varies.

The largest cost component identifies a target worth evaluating. The most effective intervention also depends on what it costs to change that component. Token pricing governs the largest cost term only when inference charges dominate the task account. In many production systems, the primary expense lies elsewhere: container sandboxes, test-suite execution, human review, or a depressed acceptance rate that multiplies all other costs. A rigorous cost accounting reveals this governing term before an engineering team spends resources optimizing the wrong bottleneck. Table 9 gathers the governing economic relations developed throughout this chapter, along with the assumptions under which each holds, such as cohort settlement, ideal prefix reuse, independent retries, or stationary queueing.

Key Takeaways: Price the accepted task, not the token
  • Acceptance divides the whole bill: A token discount reduces only the model’s direct share of an attempt, whereas the acceptance rate \(\AcceptanceFraction\) scales every operational expense. In addition, each successful task must absorb the amortized cost of \((1-\AcceptanceFraction)/\AcceptanceFraction\) nonaccepted tasks. In the repair cohort, the configuration with the lower nominal token price ultimately cost 2.4× as much per accepted repair.
  • Reuse and retries are investments, not free savings: Prefix caching reduces the slope of quadratic context growth but does not eliminate the re-read penalty. Context compaction pays off only after surviving beyond its payback horizon \(\CompactionPaybackCallCount\). Similarly, when a human fallback resolves every ticket the loop leaves, an additional execution turn makes economic sense only when the fallback spend it expects to avoid, \(\StageConditionalAcceptanceProbability_t\HumanFallbackCost\), exceeds the full cost of that turn.
  • Speed pays only on the critical path: Accelerating a model component shortens only its own spans along the trajectory’s execution graph; generation acceleration acts on decode spans. In the hypothetical serial repair episode, a 4× model speedup yielded only a 1.08× end-to-end speedup for the overall episode, because sandbox lifecycle operations and test suite runs formed the dominant latency floor.
  • A cascade is only as cheap as its gates: Tiered routing saves money only when every exit tier fulfills the identical acceptance contract. If an upstream gate erroneously accepts invalid outputs, even a tiny false-acceptance rate can completely erase the cascade’s cost advantage once downstream remediation and task failure costs enter the balance sheet.
  • Utilization is paid for in queueing delay: System throughput scales linearly with load, but mean queue wait grows nonlinearly as \(\ServingUtilization / (1 - \ServingUtilization)\). Because an agent trajectory pays this queueing latency across every sequential step, operating at excessive utilization quickly destroys interactive responsiveness. Moreover, falling acceptance forces the runtime to admit \(1/\AcceptanceFraction\) tasks per accepted outcome to sustain target goodput, directly coupling capacity planning to task reliability.
  • A spending limit holds only if reserved first: Admission control must atomically reserve an enforceable bound before execution begins, releasing unused funds only upon task settlement. Because terminating a child process does not instantly extinguish its pending provider billing, runtime tracking must strictly separate settled costs from in-flight liabilities. Monitoring burn rates merely observes cost overruns after the fact; strict upfront reservation prevents them.
  • The largest term identifies a target to evaluate: Trajectory horizon, working state size, and external authority determine which subsystem dominates execution cost. Token generation dominates in deep research agents, sandbox holding in code repair, human review delays in infrastructure remediation, and cross-system handoffs in customer workflows. An optimization that cuts total cost in half for one agent architecture may yield negligible savings for another.

Evaluating an agent system by trajectory goodput (principle 15) establishes a financial metric, the effective cost per accepted task, \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\), and its operational reciprocal, goodput per dollar. This principle requires system designers to account for resource residence and queueing overheads rather than focusing solely on active generation. In parallel, trajectory-level encapsulation (principle 10) preserves spending discipline across child tasks through two-phase reservation and settlement, maintaining a separation between reusable operational capabilities and consumable financial budgets.

19.12 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

The principles governing execution time and capacity planning in agent workflows trace directly back to mid-twentieth-century operations research. When project planners needed to isolate which activities determined an overall delivery date, Kelley and Walker introduced the critical-path method to identify the longest chain of dependent tasks (Kelley and Walker 1959). Queueing theory formalized the operational limits of shared capacity. Little proved that mean occupancy equals arrival rate multiplied by mean residence time under remarkably general conditions (Little 1961). In the same year, Kingman showed that waiting times in a single-server queue diverge nonlinearly, in proportion to \(1/(1-\ServingUtilization)\) as utilization approaches unity (Kingman 1961). Kleinrock later synthesized these results into the queueing theory of computer systems (Kleinrock 1975). When early parallel computers promised order-of-magnitude speedups, Amdahl demonstrated that serial bottlenecks strictly bound the end-to-end performance gains achievable by accelerating isolated subroutines (Amdahl 1967). Each generation encountered the same dilemma confronting modern repair agents: placing a fast component inside a slow end-to-end pipeline yields little return unless engineers account for the performance of the system as a whole.

Kelley, James E., and Morgan R. Walker. 1959. “Critical-Path Planning and Scheduling.” Papers Presented at the December 1-3, 1959, Eastern Joint IRE-AIEE-ACM Computer Conference, 160–73. https://doi.org/10.1145/1460299.1460318.
Little, John D. C. 1961. “A Proof for the Queuing Formula: \(L = \lambda W\).” Operations Research 9 (3): 383–87. https://doi.org/10.1287/opre.9.3.383.
Kleinrock, L. 1975. Queueing Systems, Volume 1: Theory. Wiley-Interscience.
Amdahl, Gene M. 1967. “Validity of the Single Processor Approach to Achieving Large Scale Computing Capabilities.” Proceedings of the April 18-20, 1967, Spring Joint Computer Conference on - AFIPS ’67 (Spring), AFIPS ’67 (spring), 483–85. https://doi.org/10.1145/1465482.1465560.
Gray, Jim, and Andreas Reuter. 1992. Transaction Processing: Concepts and Techniques. Morgan Kaufmann.

The economics of budget enforcement and resource consumption descend from transaction processing and utility computing. High-concurrency database systems facing contention on hot account balances introduced escrow transactions to allocate fractional quantities without exclusive record locks (O’Neil 1986). This gave an early model for concurrent budget reservations within the framework of atomic, durable, and recoverable updates (Gray and Reuter 1992). When compute resources transitioned to on-demand utility pricing, the trade-off between renting vs. owning infrastructure turned on workload variability, average capacity utilization, and which party absorbs provisioning risk (Armbrust et al. 2010). As distributed systems expanded across warehouse-scale infrastructure, Dean and Barroso demonstrated that tail latency across parallel RPCs quickly dominates overall response times (Dean and Barroso 2013). This is the parallel counterpart of how a multi-step agent trajectory compounds tool and service variability in series.

Machine learning contributed its own lineage of allocating computation adaptively based on instance difficulty. Viola and Jones demonstrated that a cascade of boosted classifiers could deliver real-time visual detection by rejecting straightforward negative examples in early stages (Viola and Jones 2001). That design pattern reemerged for foundation models in the form of cost-aware cascades and learned routers that dispatch queries across heterogeneous model endpoints (L. Chen et al. 2023; Ong et al. 2024). Similarly, speculative execution evolved from blockwise parallel decoding (Stern et al. 2018) into speculative decoding frameworks that pair small draft models with full target-model verification (Leviathan et al. 2023; C. Chen et al. 2023). In agentic workflows, repeated sampling strategies showed that additional generation attempts reliably expand solution coverage whenever verifiers can recognize task success (Brown et al. 2024). These results highlighted a key requirement for evaluating production agents: benchmark assessments must report task accuracy alongside cumulative expenditure, establishing cost per accepted task as the central engineering metric (Kapoor et al. 2024).

Viola, Paul, and Michael J. Jones. 2001. “Rapid Object Detection Using a Boosted Cascade of Simple Features.” Proceedings of the 2001 IEEE Computer Society Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition. CVPR 2001 1: I-511-I-518. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr.2001.990517.
Chen, Lingjiao, Matei Zaharia, and James Zou. 2023. “FrugalGPT: How to Use Large Language Models While Reducing Cost and Improving Performance.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2305.05176.
Ong, Isaac, Amjad Almahairi, Vincent Wu, Wei-Lin Chiang, Tianhao Wu, Joseph E. Gonzalez, M. Waleed Kadous, and Ion Stoica. 2024. “RouteLLM: Learning to Route LLMs with Preference Data.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2406.18665.
Leviathan, Yaniv, Matan Kalman, and Yossi Matias. 2023. “Fast Inference from Transformers via Speculative Decoding.” Proceedings of the 40th International Conference on Machine Learning 202: 19274–86.
Chen, C., S. Borgeaud, G. Irving, J.-B. Lespiau, L. Sifre, and J. Jumper. 2023. “Accelerating Large Language Model Decoding with Speculative Sampling.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2302.01318.
Brown, Bradley, Jordan Juravsky, Ryan Ehrlich, Ronald Clark, Quoc V. Le, Christopher Ré, and Azalia Mirhoseini. 2024. “Large Language Monkeys: Scaling Inference Compute with Repeated Sampling.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2407.21787.

Further reading.

  • The Single Server Queue in Heavy Traffic (Kingman 1961). Three pages demonstrating why waiting time, rather than reduced throughput, is the steep price of operating near full capacity.
